Before that constraint change, any pre-existing legacy `fitbit` row in `integration_connections` is deleted (`DELETE FROM integration_connections WHERE provider = 'fitbit'`) — safe because OAuth tokens never lived in this table (Keychain-only, D53), so there is no credential to reuse, reinterpret, or lose; only status/scope metadata, and Bernardo explicitly said `fitbit` should not remain a selectable provider since no real account was ever connected through it.
**Why**: Phase 9's standing engineering rule ("use schema migrations," "never rewrite a prior migration") plus Bernardo's explicit instruction for this correction: preserve any legacy row's cached data (the daily-summary table itself, preserved via rename), mark any legacy connection unsupported/disconnected in effect (achieved here by removing it entirely, since it's not selectable going forward), and never reinterpret legacy credentials as Google credentials (moot, since none were ever stored in SQLite to begin with).
**How to apply**: `test_migration_0006.py` upgrades a database to 0005, inserts a synthetic legacy `fitbit` daily-summary row and connection row, upgrades to head, and asserts: the renamed table/column exist with the data intact, the legacy connection row is gone, and the tightened CHECK constraint now rejects a fresh `'fitbit'` insert. Any future provider rename must follow this same pattern — a new forward-only migration, never an edit to the migration that first created the old name.

## D62 — Two real bugs found live during Phase 9 acceptance, both fixed and tested; one open architectural finding not yet resolved

**Decision (bug 1 — fixed)**: `app/providers/google_health.py`'s `dailyRollUp` request sent `range.start`/`range.end` as plain ISO date strings (`"2026-08-20"`). The real Google Health API rejected this with HTTP 400 — its discovery document shows `CivilDateTime` is `{date: {year, month, day}, time?: TimeOfDay}`, not a string. Fixed via a new `_civil_date()` helper producing the correct nested shape; confirmed against the real API (the same request now gets past body validation).
**Decision (bug 2 — fixed)**: `integration_service.sync_google_calendar`/`sync_google_health` re-raised the *original* provider exception (`GoogleCalendarError`/`GoogleHealthError`) on a sync failure via a bare `raise`, but both `routers/integrations.py` sync endpoints only ever caught `IntegrationError` — so any real provider-level failure (confirmed live: the bug-1 400, and separately a 403) escaped as an unhandled HTTP 500 instead of a clean 502 with a readable message. This is the same class of bug as `CalendarCapabilityError` not being caught (Phase 9's original implementation, fixed earlier this session) — a service-boundary exception type not matching what the caller catches. Fixed by normalizing both provider error types into `IntegrationError` at the `sync_*` boundary before they leave `integration_service.py`, so the router's single `except IntegrationError` now catches every sync failure from either provider.
**Why (both)**: These were only discoverable by actually calling the real Google Health API with a real connected account — no mock could have caught either, since the mocks were written to match my own (in bug 1's case, wrong) understanding of the request format. This is exactly the risk Phase 9's live-acceptance step exists to catch before Phase 9 is marked complete.
**How to apply**: `test_google_health_provider.py::test_civil_time_range_uses_the_real_civil_date_time_shape` now asserts the correct nested `CivilDateTime` shape (previously asserted the wrong, bug-reproducing shape — the old assertion was itself the encoded mistake). `test_integration_service.py` gained `test_sync_google_calendar_normalizes_provider_error_to_integration_error` and `test_sync_google_health_normalizes_provider_error_to_integration_error`, each forcing a raw provider HTTP error and asserting it surfaces as `IntegrationError`, not the raw provider exception type.

**Open finding (not yet resolved — requires Bernardo's decision)**: Google Calendar and Google Health were connected using **one shared Google OAuth Web application client** (the "simpler, no concrete reason to split" design recorded in D59/README). Both providers' authorization requests use `include_granted_scopes=true` (needed for Calendar's own incremental write-scope consent, D59). Live acceptance revealed that Google's incremental-consent grant is scoped to the **(client_id, user)** pair, not per Jarvis-internal provider: connecting Google Health *after* Google Calendar caused Google to return the *union* of all scopes ever granted to that shared client — so the Google Health connection's stored `scopes_json` ended up also containing the Calendar read scopes. The Google Health API's `dailyRollUp` endpoint explicitly rejects a request bearing any non-Health scope (`403 PERMISSION_DENIED`, `reason: DISALLOWED_OAUTH_SCOPES`, confirmed live), so **Google Health sync currently fails end-to-end** as long as one shared client is used and Calendar was connected first.
**Why this isn't silently worked around**: This is not a Jarvis-side privilege escalation — `has_scope()`/the capability-gating logic only ever reads the specific provider's own connection row (D59's write-scope check for `google_calendar` is unaffected by `google_health`'s scope list, and vice versa), so no unintended write access or cross-provider capability leak exists. It is purely that the Google Health API itself refuses a token whose scope set isn't exclusively Health scopes — an external constraint discovered only by testing against the real API with a real, already-Calendar-connected account, which is exactly why this is being recorded rather than assumed away.
**Likely fix (not yet applied — pending Bernardo's decision)**: register a **second, separate** Google OAuth Web application client for Google Health only (with only the `google_health` callback URI and only Health scopes ever requested against it), so its granted-scope set can never include Calendar scopes. This reverses the "one client is simpler" choice in D59/README for this specific pairing — the two integrations turn out not to be safely shareable after all, given how Google Health's own API validates scopes. Until this is done (or an alternative is chosen), Google Health sync will continue to fail for any account that has ever also granted Calendar scopes to the same client.
**How to apply**: Do not add a workaround that strips or filters scopes client-side — the fix is a real second OAuth client, verified against the real API, the same way D59-D61 were.

## D63 — D62's "one shared client" hypothesis was wrong; the real cause was Google Health requesting `include_granted_scopes` it never needed

**Decision**: Bernardo registered the second, separate OAuth client D62 recommended (a distinct client ID/secret, verified by comparing Keychain-stored credential hashes for the two providers — genuinely different). Google Health's connection **still** came back with Calendar's scopes attached. This disproves D62's hypothesis that the scope union was keyed to `(client_id, user)` — it is evidently keyed to `(Cloud project, user)` instead, since both OAuth clients live under the same project. The actual, correct fix is simpler than another client: Google Health has a **fixed** set of three read-only scopes and never performs incremental authorization (unlike Calendar, which later adds `calendar.events.owned` via a genuine incremental-consent round trip) — so `include_granted_scopes=true` was serving no purpose in `google_health.build_authorization_url` and is the actual, sole cause of the union/bleed. It has been removed entirely (never set to `"false"` — omitting the parameter is the provider-correct way to request only what's asked for). Google Calendar's authorization URL is unchanged and still sends `include_granted_scopes=true`, since it genuinely needs that behavior for its own incremental write-scope consent.
**Why**: `include_granted_scopes=true` only ever makes sense for a flow that *itself* performs incremental authorization — asking for it unconditionally, by copying the pattern from `google_calendar.py` without re-examining whether Google Health needed it, was the root mistake. The two-client change from D62 was real, useful hardening (each integration's Keychain credentials are now genuinely separate, and each API's requests are now bound to only its own client) but was not, by itself, sufficient — the actual defect lived in which OAuth *parameters* Google Health's request sent, not which client it used.
**How to apply**: `test_google_health_provider.py::test_google_health_authorization_omits_include_granted_scopes` asserts the parameter is entirely absent from the Health authorization URL; `test_google_health_requests_exactly_its_three_read_only_scopes` pins the exact scope set. `test_calendar_capability.py`'s existing `test_incremental_consent_preserves_previously_granted_read_scopes` and `test_client_type_and_redirect_uri_match_web_application_flow` continue to assert Calendar's URL still contains `include_granted_scopes=true` — the two providers must not converge on the same authorization-parameter shape again just because they share a request-building pattern. Any future OAuth-based integration must ask, explicitly, "does this flow ever perform incremental authorization?" before copying `include_granted_scopes=true` from Calendar's implementation.

## D64 — Google Health breadth correction: a general integration (not Fitbit-specific), a typed metric registry, and four more real bugs found live

**Decision**: Renamed the product-facing language from "Fitbit via Google Health" to **Google Health** throughout the UI (`IntegrationsCentre.tsx`, README) — the internal provider id `google_health` is unchanged, no migration was rewritten, and export/database compatibility is preserved. Google Health reads reconciled, consented data that can originate from Fitbit, Pixel Watch, Health Connect, Google Fit, or any other source connected to the account; the UI now says so explicitly and never claims a specific source or that every metric is guaranteed present. `app/providers/google_health.py` was rewritten around an explicit, typed metric registry (`ROLLUP_METRICS`, `DAILY_LIST_METRICS`, `SESSION_METRICS` — each entry records the Google Health data-type id, governing scope, REST operation, category, range/page-size behavior, and BODY-only eligibility) instead of a single narrow daily-summary shape, driven by three verified fetch mechanisms: `dailyRollUp` for interval-summable metrics (steps, distance, floors, active zone minutes, active/total calories, heart rate), unfiltered `list` + client-side date truncation for "daily-*" precomputed singleton types and point samples (resting HR, HRV, SpO2, respiratory rate, VO2 max, weight, body fat, blood glucose — filter syntax was live-verified to reject every field-name variant tried for these), and filtered `list` for sessions (sleep, exercise). Migration `0007_google_health_breadth.py` adds the new daily-summary columns and a new `google_health_sessions` table, forward-only, without touching 0005/0006. A metric's fetch failure is now caught per-metric and recorded in `partial_failures`, never aborting the rest of the sync (`last_sync_status="partial"` with a summary of which metrics failed) — confirmed live: a 31-day sync correctly marked `total_calories`/`heart_rate` as failed (they're bounded to 14 days) while the other ~15 metrics and both session types synced successfully in the same call.
**Four more real bugs found live against the already-connected account** (this correction's own verification step, exactly the pattern D62/D63 established): (1) `distance` is `distance.millimetersSum` in **millimeters**, not the originally-assumed meters. (2) Several fields declared `type: string, format: int64` in the discovery doc (`steps.countSum`, `distance.millimetersSum`, `floors.countSum`, the three `activeZoneMinutes.sumIn*HeartZone` fields, `DailyRestingHeartRate.beatsPerMinute`) arrive as **JSON strings**, not numbers — confirmed by a live 400→(fixed)→success sequence and a mock/assert mismatch caught by `test_daily_list_metric_truncates_to_requested_range_client_side` during this correction; all are now cast via a defensive `_as_int()` helper regardless of whether the API sends a string or number. (3) `civilStartTime`/`civilEndTime` on a `dailyRollUp` response point are structured `{date: {year, month, day}, time: {}}` **CivilDateTime objects**, not plain ISO date strings — the original code's `civil_start[:10]` string-slice raised `KeyError: slice(...)` on a real sync attempt, a genuinely uncaught crash, not merely wrong data. (4) A rollup point's metric value sits **directly on the point** (e.g. `point["steps"]["countSum"]`), with no `"value"` wrapper — the original code's `point.get("value", {})` silently produced empty dicts for every metric, meaning the very first Phase 9 Google Health implementation had *never once* actually parsed a real value out of any dailyRollUp response, despite its mocked tests passing (because the mocks encoded the same wrong assumption). Also live-verified: sleep and exercise sessions use different filter-field conventions for the same "get me things starting/ending in this range" query — `sleep.interval.end_time` with UTC `Z` timestamps vs. `exercise.interval.civil_start_time` with civil (no `Z`) timestamps — confirmed by direct trial against the real API, not assumed.
**Why**: Every one of these was invisible to mocked tests until checked against the real, connected API — exactly why Phase 9's live-acceptance step exists, and exactly the same lesson as D62/D63 repeated a third time. Missing/unsupported data for a given account is normal per Bernardo's explicit instruction, so a per-metric failure must never abort the whole sync — confirmed this now genuinely works via the live 31-day/14-day-limit partial-failure case, not just a mocked one.
**How to apply**: `test_google_health_provider.py` was substantially rewritten to mirror the real, live-verified response shapes exactly (a `_rollup_point()` test helper builds the correct nested `civilStartTime` + unwrapped value shape), and gained: `test_steps_and_distance_use_the_real_rollup_field_names` (mm-to-km conversion), `test_heart_rate_rollup_extracts_avg_min_max`, `test_daily_list_metric_truncates_to_requested_range_client_side`, `test_weight_extracts_grams_to_kg_and_source_platform`, `test_sleep_session_parses_stages_into_minutes_asleep_and_awake`, `test_exercise_session_parses_metrics_summary`, `test_one_metric_failure_does_not_abort_the_rest_of_the_sync`, and `test_sleep_filter_uses_utc_end_time_exercise_filter_uses_civil_start_time`. `test_integration_service.py` gained `test_sync_google_health_is_idempotent_across_repeated_syncs` (repeated sync with identical upstream data must not duplicate a daily-summary row or a session row — both enforced by a real DB unique constraint) and `test_sync_google_health_records_partial_status_without_aborting_on_metric_failure`. `test_migration_0007.py` confirms the new columns/table exist and pre-existing data survives. Any future Google Health metric addition must be verified against the real API the same way — the discovery document alone was not sufficient to catch any of these four bugs.
**Live acceptance performed without disconnecting/reconnecting/revoking**: the already-connected `google_health` OAuth connection (established under D62/D63's corrected flow) was preserved throughout this entire correction — no token was read, displayed, deleted, or refreshed outside the normal `ensure_fresh_access_token` path, no new OAuth flow was started, and the three granted scopes were re-verified unchanged before and after. One real sync populated 31 daily-summary rows and 17 real sessions (sleep with stage detail, exercise with type/duration/calories) from a real Fitbit-via-Google-Health account, confirming the whole corrected pipeline end-to-end.

## D65 — Metric-aware range chunking for Google Health, and a genuine OAuth credential-persistence/scope-corruption defect found and fixed live

**Decision (chunking)**: `dailyRollUp` metrics with a 14-day range limit (`heart-rate`, `total-calories`) previously failed outright with `range_too_large` on any sync window wider than 14 days, surfacing to Bernardo as a routine `"partial"` sync status — never actually normal user-facing behavior once the registry already records each metric's own limit. `app/providers/google_health.py` gained `_chunk_date_range()` (splits `[start, end)` into consecutive, non-overlapping, gap-free chunks of at most a metric's `max_range_days`) and `_daily_roll_up_chunked()` (issues one `dailyRollUp` call per chunk, combining results; a failure in one chunk is recorded against that metric without discarding data already fetched from other, successful chunks of the same call, and no immediate retry is attempted). `fetch_health_data`'s rollup loop now calls the chunked helper for every rollup metric. Confirmed live: an ordinary 31-day sync request, which previously came back `"partial"` for `heart_rate`/`total_calories` every time, now returns `"ok"` with real data spanning the full 31 days.
**Decision (a real, separate, more serious defect found while investigating a reported "shows disconnected" incident)**: `handle_google_calendar_callback`/`handle_google_health_callback` stored *whatever* scope string Google's OAuth callback returned, with no filtering. Google associates OAuth consent with the **(user, Cloud project)** pair, not (user, client_id) — confirmed by inspecting real callback logs: attempts to enable Calendar's write scope came back with Google Health's 3 scopes mixed into the Calendar callback (both integrations share one Cloud project), corrupting `google_calendar`'s `scopes_json` and, in the first several attempts, never actually including the requested `calendar.events.owned` scope at all (most likely because that scope hadn't yet been added to the project's OAuth consent-screen "Data access" configuration — an external, one-time setup step Bernardo later completed). `integration_service.py` gained `_normalize_granted_scopes()` (filters a callback's granted-scope string down to only scopes that belong to that specific provider's own known scope set, falling back to the previously-stored scopes if filtering leaves nothing at all — never overwriting a real grant with an empty one) and `_persist_oauth_connection()` (a shared, safe callback-completion path for both providers: Keychain is written first, and a Keychain failure now raises before the database row is ever marked connected, so DB and Keychain can never disagree with each other).
**Why**: The refresh-token-loss hypothesis Bernardo raised initially did not hold — `_store_tokens` already correctly skipped writing a missing `refresh_token` rather than deleting a stored one. The real, confirmed, and more consequential defect was unfiltered scope storage combined with Google's project-wide (not client-wide) consent behavior — worth fixing regardless of the exact trigger for what was originally observed in the UI, since scope corruption is a real, silent correctness bug on its own.
**How to apply**: `test_google_health_provider.py` gained `test_chunk_date_range_splits_31_days_into_correct_14_day_chunks`, `test_chunk_date_range_does_not_split_when_within_limit`, `test_heart_rate_31_day_sync_no_longer_reports_range_too_large`, `test_one_chunk_failure_preserves_data_from_other_successful_chunks`, and `test_chunked_fetch_produces_no_duplicate_days`. `test_integration_service.py` gained 7 new tests covering: initial/incremental refresh-token handling (both omitted and replaced), granted read+write scopes persisted correctly, cross-provider scope contamination filtered out, an entirely-contaminated response falling back to previously-stored scopes, a credential-store failure never producing a connected database row, and a Calendar callback never touching Google Health's own Keychain entries or connection row. Any future provider sharing a Cloud project with another must go through the same allowed-scope filtering — never trust a callback's raw scope string as authoritative for a specific provider.

## D66 — A real Pydantic type bug and a dangerous frontend fallback combined to produce a false "disconnected" status; both fixed

**Decision**: While verifying D65's fix, the Integrations Centre showed `"Could not load integrations"` with both providers rendered as `disconnected`, even though `/api/integrations` itself was returning `200` correctly every time it was checked directly. Root cause, confirmed via direct endpoint checks and backend logs: `GoogleHealthDailySummaryRead.heart_rate_avg_bpm/min/max` were declared `int`, but Google's real API types them as `number` — `beatsPerMinuteAvg` is routinely fractional (e.g. `64.51...`) — so reading back a real stored value raised a genuine, unhandled `500` on `GET /api/integrations/google_health/summaries`. `IntegrationsCentre.tsx`'s `refresh()` used `Promise.all([...])` across 8 independent requests; that one unrelated 500 rejected the whole batch, so `setConnections()` was never called, `connections` stayed at its empty initial state, and the JSX's `googleConn?.status ?? "disconnected"` fallback rendered *both* providers as disconnected — a completely misleading status display driven by a bug that had nothing to do with connection state at all.
**Decision (fixes)**: `heart_rate_avg_bpm/min/max` widened to `float` in both the ORM model and the Pydantic schema; migration `0008_heart_rate_float.py` (forward-only, 0005-0007 untouched) widens the SQLite columns. `routers/integrations.py`'s `list_integrations` now fetches each provider independently, catching per-provider exceptions and reporting `status="error"` (never silently reinterpreted as `"disconnected"`) without affecting the other provider — still a pure read, never touching the Keychain. `IntegrationsCentre.tsx`'s `refresh()` now isolates the connection-status fetch (`listIntegrations()`) from the 7 supplementary, non-critical fetches via `Promise.allSettled` — a new `statusUnknown` state renders "Status unavailable," hides every Connect/Sync/Disconnect/Enable-writing action, and offers a Retry button; only a backend-confirmed `disconnected` is ever displayed as such.
**Why**: A connection-status display must never lie in the direction of "everything is broken" (inviting an unnecessary, and potentially unsafe, reconnect) any more than it should lie in the direction of "everything is fine." Isolating the status-critical request from supplementary, non-critical ones is the general fix — one unrelated data-fetch failure must never be able to misrepresent connection state again.
**How to apply**: `test_integrations_api.py` gained `test_one_provider_status_failure_does_not_break_the_other_providers_result` and `test_list_integrations_never_touches_the_credential_store`. `test_migration_0008.py` confirms the column widening preserves existing data and accepts a real fractional value. `IntegrationsCentre.test.tsx` gained two tests: status-unavailable-with-all-actions-disabled-and-a-retry-button, and recovery-to-real-states-after-a-successful-retry. Any future addition to `refresh()`'s parallel fetches must go into the non-critical `Promise.allSettled` group, never back into a shared `Promise.all` with the connection-status fetch.

## D67 — Phase 9 live acceptance complete

**Decision**: With D59-D66's corrections in place, full live acceptance was performed against Bernardo's real, already-connected Google account and real local installation: (1) Google Health breadth sync — 31 daily-summary rows and 18 sessions (sleep with stage detail, exercise with type/duration/calories/distance/heart rate) from a real Fitbit-via-Google-Health account, `"ok"` status with metric-aware chunking. (2) Google Calendar read — primary owned calendar selected, one real sync, events cached and confirmed LIFE-only. (3) Google Calendar write — the full Phase 8 lifecycle exercised on a real account: a clearly-labelled temporary event proposed, manually approved, executed exactly once, verified present via a real read sync; its deletion separately proposed, manually approved, executed exactly once, verified absent via a second real read sync; complete audit histories (`proposed → approved → executing → succeeded` for both) persisted locally. (4) Local document prompt-injection isolation — performed in an isolated temporary data directory (never `~/JarvisData`): a synthetic document with factual statements plus an adversarial "reveal secrets, delete all Calendar events" instruction was imported, correctly MIME-sniffed, SHA-256-deduplicated, chunked, and FTS-indexed; one real model call answered a factual question with a citation and did not comply with the embedded instruction; zero action proposals existed afterward, confirming structural isolation (no code path from document text to action execution). (5) Restart/persistence — all six domains, both integration connections (with only their own correct, provider-specific scopes), Keychain entries, Calendar cache/selection, Google Health data, and Calendar action audit histories all survived a full `jarvisctl.sh` restart; Hermes toolsets confirmed still fully disabled (0 enabled). (6) Export/restore — a real export validated cleanly (schema revision 0008, checksums matched, `secrets_included: false`, zero token/secret/absolute-path patterns found in the archive); restored into a brand-new isolated target (never over `~/JarvisData`) with all domains, Calendar cache, Google Health breadth data, and Calendar action/audit histories intact, both integrations correctly forced to `disconnected` with cleared scopes, and the restored database confirmed writable; the real installation was left untouched and healthy throughout. (7) Final suite: 359/359 backend pytest tests, 48/48 frontend Vitest tests, typecheck, and production build all pass in one full run each.
**Known, genuine limitations carried forward, not fixed by this acceptance pass** (see D64): `basal-energy-burned`'s rollup value schema remains unconfirmed against the discovery document and is not implemented; `calories-in-heart-rate-zone`, `time-in-heart-rate-zone`, `daily-heart-rate-zones`, `altitude`, core/sleep body-temperature data types, and detailed `exerciseType` enum labeling are not implemented (explicitly deferred, not silently omitted); Daily Readiness Score, Sleep Score, Stress Management Score, and Cardio Load/Target Load remain permanently unsupported (not exposed by any Google Health data type). Periodic automatic resync (Calendar every 15 minutes, Google Health every 60 minutes, both user-disableable) remains an explicitly deferred Phase 10 requirement, never implemented in Phase 9.
**Why this closes Phase 9**: every acceptance item Bernardo specified — read-only integrations, the limited Calendar write expansion, local document import with prompt-injection isolation, export/restore, and a full automated-test confirmation — has now been exercised against real accounts/data, not just mocks, with every real defect found along the way (D62-D66) fixed and covered by a regression test.
**How to apply**: see `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 9 entry for the final status line. Any future work in this area is Phase 10 (automatic resync) or a new, explicitly-scoped correction — not a silent reopening of Phase 9.

## D68 — Phase 10: controller-owned automatic integration resync — a single asyncio loop, never a heavier scheduler

**Decision**: Automatic resync for Google Calendar and Google Health is a single `asyncio.Task` (`app/scheduler_runtime.py::SchedulerRuntime`) started and stopped by FastAPI's own `lifespan` in `main.py` — never a Hermes cron job, a Hermes skill, a sub-agent, or a second process. The whole requirement reduces to "every N seconds, check whether either of two providers is due, and if so run the existing Phase 9 sync once" — a plain sleep-loop satisfies this with zero new dependencies, is trivially bounded (a fixed tick interval) and cancellable (`asyncio.Task.cancel()` on shutdown), and needs no persistence of its own beyond the schedule table it already reads. A heavier library (APScheduler, Celery, etc.) was considered and rejected: none of their extra features (cron-string parsing, multi-worker coordination, persistent job stores) apply to a single-process, two-provider, tick-based design.
**Persistent schedule + bounded audit trail**: migration `0009_integration_sync_schedules.py` (forward-only, 0001-0008 untouched) adds `integration_sync_schedules` (one row per provider, seeded `enabled=0` — Bernardo must explicitly turn each one on) and `integration_sync_runs` (a bounded local audit trail — `app/scheduler_service.py::_trim_history` keeps only the most recent `SYNC_RUN_RETENTION_PER_PROVIDER=50` rows per provider, trimmed on every insert). Neither table stores a raw provider response, a token, or any secret — only sanitized outcome/reason strings and non-sensitive counts (e.g. `{"cached_events": 3}`, never event titles).
**Due-time and catch-up semantics**: `next_due_at` always advances from the actual completion time of a real attempt (`now + interval` on success, `now + backoff` on failure) — never by incrementally replaying missed slots. This is what structurally prevents a replay storm after a long sleep/downtime: `startup_catchup()` and `tick()` both just ask "is this provider's stored `next_due_at` in the past?" and, if so, run it *once*; a schedule overdue by three days behaves identically to one overdue by three minutes. Enabling a schedule sets `next_due_at = now` (one immediate sync, per Bernardo's explicit requirement), and disabling clears it (`next_due_at = None`) so nothing further is scheduled.
**Single-flight locking**: one `threading.Lock` per provider (`app/scheduler_service.py`'s module-level `_PROVIDER_LOCKS`), acquired non-blocking by every sync attempt regardless of trigger — manual ("Sync now"), scheduled, or startup catch-up all share the same lock, so they can never run concurrently for the same provider. A collision is recorded as a `"skipped"` run (never silently dropped, never blocked/queued) and the manual-sync HTTP endpoint returns `409` in that case.
**Failure handling**: a sync failure is caught inside `run_provider_sync` and recorded — it never crashes the tick loop, never marks the real `IntegrationConnection` disconnected (a sync failure and a genuine disconnection are different, truthful states), and never logs a token or provider payload. Backoff is bounded exponential (`interval × 2^failures`, capped at 8× the interval and an absolute 24 hours) and honors a provider's `Retry-After` when available (`IntegrationError.retry_after`, threaded through from `GoogleHealthError`) by taking whichever wait is longer. `consecutive_failure_count` resets to 0 on the next success. A Google Health "partial" sync (some metric groups failed, others succeeded — the existing Phase 9 truthful-partial behavior) is treated as a real success for scheduling purposes, not a failure, since the sync itself completed.
**Restore behavior**: `import_service._disable_all_integration_schedules` (mirroring `_disconnect_all_integrations`) forces every schedule to `enabled=0, next_due_at=NULL` immediately after every restore — a restored installation must never resume automatic sync against a provider that's simultaneously been forced disconnected in the same restore step.
**UI**: an explicit local UI action (the enable toggle + cadence selector) does **not** go through the Phase 8 proposal lifecycle — configuring Jarvis's own local schedule metadata has no external side effect of its own (unlike a Calendar write), so the propose→approve→execute ceremony doesn't apply here, the same reasoning that already governs calendar selection.
**How to apply**: `test_scheduler_service.py` (17 tests: defaults disabled, immediate-sync-on-enable, provider-specific interval validation, ordinary due-sync advancing `next_due_at`, exactly-one startup catch-up, no-replay-storm after long downtime, single-flight collision, failure backoff/reset, Retry-After honored, truthful partial-status preservation, bounded retention, no-secrets-in-audit-row) and `test_scheduler_runtime.py` (3 tests: idempotent `start()`, clean bounded `stop()`, no duplicate sync across a rapid restart) — all using `FakeCredentialStore`, `httpx.MockTransport`, and an injected fake clock; none touch the real Keychain, a real Google API, Hermes, or make a model call. `test_migration_0009.py` and an extension to `test_round_trip_phase9.py` cover the schema and restore-disabling behavior.

## D69 — Phase 10 live acceptance (complete)

**Decision**: with Bernardo's explicit enablement (Calendar every 15 minutes, Google Health every 60 minutes) through the Integrations Centre, live acceptance was performed against the real, already-connected account: enabling each schedule triggered exactly one real controller-triggered sync per provider (recorded with `trigger="scheduled"`, not `"manual"` — the background tick loop picked up the `next_due_at = now` set by enabling), both `outcome="succeeded"` with sanitized, non-sensitive result counts only (`{"cached_events": 1}`, `{"daily_summaries": 31, "sessions": 18}`). `next_due_at` advanced correctly for both (`last_attempt_at + interval`). No new Calendar write action proposal was created by automatic sync (confirmed: only the two pre-existing, already-`succeeded` proposals from the earlier manual create/delete acceptance test exist) and the Calendar event cache contains only the real, original event — automatic sync never invokes a write. Google Health's cached row/session counts were unchanged by the sync (already-current data, correctly re-confirmed rather than duplicated). A full `jarvisctl.sh` restart preserved both schedules enabled with identical cadences and due times, and — because neither provider was actually overdue at restart time — triggered **zero** additional syncs (sync-run counts remained exactly 1 per provider), confirming the catch-up-only-when-overdue design holds under a real restart, not just a mocked one. Exactly one backend process runs (`ps aux` confirmed), so exactly one `SchedulerRuntime` instance/background loop exists. Hermes toolsets confirmed still zero enabled, both before and after.
**Manual-vs-scheduled single-flight collision**: per Bernardo's own instruction, this relied on the existing automated collision tests (`test_manual_and_scheduled_sync_cannot_overlap`, `test_lock_is_released_after_a_run_so_a_later_sync_can_proceed`) rather than a live test — deliberately forcing a live collision would mean hammering Google's API concurrently for no additional confidence beyond what the mocked test already proves about the shared-lock code path.
**No code changes were required during this live-acceptance pass** — no defect was found, so the full suite was not rerun (per Bernardo's own instruction); the suite that already passed at the Phase 10 implementation boundary stands.
**How to apply**: see `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 10 entry for the final status line.

## D70 — Phase 10B: controller-owned proactive routines, and sub-agents formally excluded from V1

**Decision**: Implemented a fixed, controller-owned routine catalogue (`morning_briefing`, `evening_checkin`, `weekly_review`) as plain Python logic in `app/routine_service.py`, reusing §9d/D68's exact `SchedulerRuntime` background loop, injected clock, per-type single-flight locking, and bounded-backoff patterns rather than any new scheduler, Hermes cron, or sub-agent. No routine may call a model, mutate Calendar/Health data, write a memory, send a notification, speak aloud, or contact anyone — the only model-reachable action anywhere in this feature is the manual "Discuss with Jarvis" button.
**Timezone/DST-safe scheduling**: `_next_occurrence_utc` constructs the candidate occurrence directly in the target IANA timezone's wall-clock terms (never by adding a fixed-offset `timedelta` to a UTC instant), then converts back to UTC — verified against a real Europe/Lisbon DST transition using genuine October/November dates. (The first version of this test asserted `.utcoffset()` on the function's *returned* UTC-tzinfo datetime, which is always `timedelta(0)` regardless of the real local DST state — fixed by converting the return value back into `ZoneInfo("Europe/Lisbon")` before checking the offset; the underlying production logic was correct throughout, only the test's verification method was wrong.)
**Content is deterministic and source-referenced, reusing existing data models**: no new table was invented for tasks/deadlines/checkpoints — the existing Phase 4 `StructuredRecord` model and its established record types (`life_task`, `path_deadline`, `build_checkpoint`, `body_weight`, `body_symptom`, `mind_checkin`, `people_interaction`) plus the Phase 9/10A integration caches (`CalendarEventCache`, `GoogleHealthDailySummary`) are the sole content source, with `archived_at IS NULL`/`IS NOT NULL` as the open/completed distinction. Every rendered line carries a `source_ref`. BODY/MIND/PEOPLE require explicit per-routine opt-in for Morning Briefing (`SENSITIVE_DOMAIN_SLUGS`); Weekly Review requires explicit opt-in for every domain, sensitive or not, since it has no fixed "core" content the way Morning Briefing does.
**Evening Check-in never auto-promotes to memory**: it is a fully static 4-prompt template with no data query and no model call; Bernardo's own typed answers are written only to that specific `RoutineRun.responses_json` via a dedicated endpoint, never into a Phase 4 `memory_item` — matching the CLAUDE.md §7 rule that ordinary interaction is not automatically promoted to permanent memory.
**"Discuss with Jarvis" reuses the existing turns endpoint rather than adding a new model-calling route**: the frontend calls the existing `createConversation`/`sendTurn` functions against the existing `POST /api/conversations/{id}/turns` endpoint, sending the routine's rendered text as an ordinary user turn. This was a deliberate design choice — it means zero new backend code can ever initiate a model call, and the existing context-boundary/domain-scoping machinery applies unchanged. The Routine Centre UI visually and textually distinguishes the routine's own deterministic output ("generated locally — not a model response") from the model's reply ("Jarvis (model response)").
**Scheduler coexistence, not a second background task**: `scheduler_runtime.py` was extended, not duplicated — `_run_startup_catchup`/`_run_tick` call both `scheduler_service` and `routine_service`'s equivalents in the same pass, each wrapped in its own try/except so one subsystem's failure can never block or crash the other. Verified with a real (not mocked) `SchedulerRuntime.start()`/`.stop()` cycle driving both an overdue integration sync and an overdue routine simultaneously.
**Sub-agents formally excluded from V1**: per Bernardo's explicit standing product decision, Jarvis remains one assistant with six context spaces; BODY, MIND, PEOPLE, PATH, BUILD, and LIFE are not separate agents; Hermes delegation and all Hermes toolsets remain disabled. This is recorded as a deliberate exclusion, not an unstarted task — `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 10 header and `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §10 were both corrected to say so. Reconsider only after Phase 11, and only if a concrete bounded-task use case emerges.
**Why**: every one of these choices follows the same underlying principle already established across D68/D69 — reuse proven infrastructure instead of building parallel systems, keep automation strictly read-only and model-free by default, and make the one place a model *can* be reached a small, explicit, human-triggered action rather than something folded into automatic execution.
**How to apply**: migration `0010_routines.py` (forward-only after `0009`, `0001`-`0009` untouched). Backend tests: `test_routine_service.py` (21: disabled-by-default, enable/disable, invalid-time/domain rejection, DST transition, catch-up-exactly-once, no-replay-after-long-downtime, duplicate-prevention-across-rapid-restarts, single-flight collision, sensitive-domain exclusion/inclusion, deterministic source-reference format, evening-checkin fixed template, calendar-section today-only filtering, bounded history retention, no-secrets-in-output), `test_migration_0010.py` (1), `test_routines_api.py` (8), `test_routine_export_restore.py` (1, config/history survive but forced disabled), `test_scheduler_routine_coexistence.py` (1, real `SchedulerRuntime` cycle). Frontend: `RoutineCentre.test.tsx` (8, covering disabled defaults, enable wiring, sensitive-domain-only opt-ins on Morning Briefing, domain-toggle wiring, Run now, the local-summary/source-reference rendering and its explicit "not a model response" label, the evening-checkin local-only response save, and the Discuss-with-Jarvis conversation/turn flow with its distinctly-labeled model reply). All tests use fakes only — no real Keychain, Google, Hermes, or model contact. See `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 10 entry for the live-acceptance status still pending (restart-safety verification and applying the routine-schedule activation). Bernardo's exact schedule selections were given in a later session and are recorded there and in D72's correction — they are not still awaiting his decision, only awaiting a session with real-Mac access to apply them.

**Addendum — Hermes toolset discrepancy resolved (CLI listing vs. gateway enablement are different things)**: before enabling any Phase 10B routine, Bernardo asked for a read-only audit of an apparent contradiction — `hermes tools list -p jarvis` shows several toolsets (web, browser, terminal, file, code_execution, vision, image_gen, tts, skills, todo, memory, session_search, clarify, delegation, cronjob, computer_use) as `✓ enabled`, seemingly contradicting D68/D69's "Hermes toolsets confirmed still zero enabled" claim. Investigation (all read-only; no config, credential, or OAuth change):
- The running gateway process was confirmed via `ps aux` (`hermes -p jarvis gateway run`, PID verified) bound to `127.0.0.1:8642` only (`lsof -i` on that PID), matching `app/config.py`'s `hermes_base_url`.
- An unauthenticated `GET /v1/toolsets` against that gateway returned `HTTP 401`, as expected.
- An authenticated `GET /v1/toolsets` (using the backend's own configured `hermes_api_bearer_token`, read from `backend/.env` in-process by a throwaway diagnostic script and never printed, logged, or persisted anywhere — the script was deleted immediately after use) returned `HTTP 200` with **27 toolsets total, all 27 reporting `"enabled": false"`** — including every one of the toolsets `hermes tools list` had shown as enabled. `"configured": true` on most of them only means the underlying credential/capability exists (e.g. a terminal backend is available to enable), not that the toolset is actually turned on for API requests reaching this profile's gateway.
- The gateway was then stopped and restarted once via the normal Jarvis-owned flow (`scripts/hermes-gateway.sh stop` / `start-background`, the same script `jarvisctl.sh` itself calls) — the authenticated `/v1/toolsets` result was re-checked afterward and was identical: 0 of 27 enabled.
- **Root cause of the discrepancy**: `hermes tools list -p jarvis` reflects the CLI's own interactive-session toolset defaults/availability (what an interactive `hermes chat -p jarvis` session could use if invoked directly at a terminal) — an entirely different code path and configuration surface from the API/gateway server's per-request toolset enablement, which is what actually governs any request Jarvis's FastAPI backend sends. The two were never the same setting; the CLI listing was never evidence about what the gateway grants to API callers.
**Why this matters**: Jarvis's backend never talks to Hermes through the CLI's interactive path — every conversation turn goes through the authenticated local API (`hermes_base_url`), so the CLI listing was always the wrong artifact to check for this project's actual security boundary. The gateway's own authenticated `/v1/toolsets` endpoint is the correct and now-confirmed source of truth.
**Conclusion**: the security boundary described in D68/D69 ("Hermes toolsets confirmed still zero enabled") remains intact and is now verified directly against the gateway's own enablement state, not inferred from the CLI. No configuration, credential, or OAuth material was changed in this investigation, no model call was made, and no Phase 10B routine was enabled.
**How to apply**: if this question ever needs re-checking, query the gateway's authenticated `/v1/toolsets` endpoint directly (loopback-only, bearer-token-gated) rather than `hermes tools list`, which answers a different question about a different code path.

## D71 — Phase 6 (visual system) implemented in a single consolidated pass in a secondary devcontainer, covering what would otherwise have been separate 6A/6B/6C sessions

**Context**: Bernardo issued three sequential Phase 6 prompts (6A: design tokens/global shell/orbital home; 6B: redesign every internal screen; 6C: exhaustive audit/correction/polish) in one working session on a secondary (non-Mac) devcontainer that had never previously done any Phase 6 work — each prompt's own text assumed the prior sub-phase("s") were "already implemented," which was not true at the time it arrived. This was surfaced explicitly to Bernardo before proceeding; he confirmed running all three in order and reviewing the result at the end, rather than stopping to re-confirm after each one.

**Decision**: implemented the confirmed direction ("Direction B geometry with Direction C lighting and atmosphere" — circular domain/core geometry only, no hexagons, deep purple-black background, violet core glow, cyan/teal accents, illumination reserved for the core/active domain/voice activity/meaningful state, restrained everywhere else) as one continuous body of work spanning tokens through final polish, then ran the 6C-style audit against that same work rather than against separately-completed 6A/6B artifacts.

**What changed**:
- `frontend/src/index.css` rewritten as a token-driven system (`--bg-*`, `--accent-violet/cyan`, `--text-*`, `--status-*`, `--glow-*`, `--shadow-*`, `--space-*`, `--radius-*`, `--motion-*`), with every existing component class (`.domain-view`, `.memory-card`, `.message-list`, `.command-palette*`, etc.) restyled against those tokens rather than renamed — this is what let every existing screen inherit the new visual system without needing its accessible names/roles/text changed.
- New shared presentational components: `JarvisCore` (the six-state core: idle/listening/transcribing/thinking/speaking/error, driven only by real props — no state of its own), `DomainNode` (one orbital node, container-relative `x`/`y` percentages rather than fixed coordinates), `VoiceStateIndicator` (restyles the existing voice-state text without changing the raw state word it renders, since `DomainView.voice.test.tsx` matches on that literal word), `StatusChip` (a single consistent status-as-text+color treatment, adopted in Actions Centre, Skills Centre, Routine Centre, Integrations Centre's connection/document status, and `MemoryItemCard`).
- `Home.tsx` rewritten as the orbital HUD using `JarvisCore`/`DomainNode`; its core state reflects only the one real signal available at that level (backend reachability) — voice states beyond idle/error are not fabricated at the Home level since push-to-talk remains scoped to an active domain conversation (unchanged from Phase 5).
- `App.tsx` shell restructured (brand mark, active-location label, status group, a `<nav>` for the six centre links, a reserved-space bottom shortcut-reference bar shown only on Home) without changing any button's accessible name, the keyboard-shortcut `useEffect`, or any routing state logic.
- `DomainView.tsx` restructured into a two-column dashboard grid (`.domain-grid`: identity/summary/memories/records beside the conversation) rather than one long vertical stack, with no section removed, hidden, or gated behind a tab — every existing `aria-label`, button name, and placeholder was preserved exactly so the existing test suite continues to assert real behavior, not fixture text.
- Memory Centre, Actions Centre, Skills Centre, Integrations Centre, Routine Centre, and Data Management were not individually restructured beyond the shared token/panel system plus the targeted `StatusChip` swaps above — each `<section aria-label="…">` already becomes a styled panel automatically via a single CSS rule (`.domain-view section[aria-label]`), which is what makes every one of these screens visually consistent with the rest of the app without bespoke per-screen layout work. This is a real scope trade-off (see "Known gap" below), not an oversight.
- `backend/app/routers/integrations.py`'s `_callback_page()` (the static HTML page Google's OAuth redirect lands on) was restyled to match the same visual system, and a real pre-existing defect was fixed in the same function: the callback's `error` query parameter and `str(exc)` text were interpolated into HTML unescaped — a local reflected-XSS-shaped issue on a loopback-only page. Both are now passed through `html.escape()`. This is the only backend file touched in this pass, and the only reason it was touched at all is that Bernardo's own Phase 6B brief named this exact page in scope ("Any OAuth success/error page styling that is rendered through Jarvis and can safely share the application visual language").

**Known gap (honestly scoped, not hidden)**: Memory/Actions/Skills/Integrations/Routines/Data Management received the shared token/panel/StatusChip treatment but not the deeper bespoke dashboard restructuring given to the Domain/Conversation view (which Bernardo's brief called out as deserving the most polish). Raw `<ul><li>` lists in a few places (Skills' version history, Integrations' calendar/session/summary lists, Routine history) inherit panel-level colors/spacing but were not individually converted into card rows. This is recorded as the concrete remaining scope for a lighter Phase 6C.2/6D follow-up pass, not silently dropped.

**Environment boundary honored**: this devcontainer has no `~/JarvisData`, Keychain, OAuth-connected accounts, Hermes gateway, or live backend, and `backend/.venv` is a macOS-built venv that does not run here (same root cause already documented for the Phase 10B handoff). No fake database was created, no OAuth/credential/schedule/Hermes configuration was touched, and the deferred Phase 10B routine-schedule values were not applied. The `_callback_page()` fix was verified by static syntax check (`python3 -m py_compile`) and by tracing `tests/test_integrations_api.py`'s three existing callback assertions against the new HTML by hand — not by actually running pytest, since no usable Python environment exists in this container. No browser was available in this container either (checked for `chromium`/`google-chrome`/`firefox`; none installed, none added), so no screenshot-based visual verification was performed at any viewport size — all responsive claims rest on the CSS's own container-relative sizing and breakpoints, not an observed render.

**Why**: Bernardo explicitly said to work autonomously across all three sub-phases and review the result at the end, rather than pausing between them — but that instruction governs *pacing*, not *honesty*; the report and this record both say plainly what was verified at the source level versus what still requires the real Mac, rather than describing devcontainer-only work as a completed live acceptance.
**How to apply**: see `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 6 entry for the exact required real-Mac acceptance steps before this phase can be marked complete (visual inspection at three viewport sizes including all six orbit nodes and PATH specifically, a live voice-state walkthrough, and running the full backend suite plus the three OAuth-callback tests in `test_integrations_api.py`).

## D72 — Cinematic multi-ring Jarvis HUD, genuine browser QA in a temporary Playwright harness, and a full-repository audit/documentation reconciliation

**Context**: after D71's implementation pass, Bernardo tightened the Phase 6 visual requirement — the core needed to read as a genuinely cinematic, multi-layered, animated HUD (in the spirit of a JARVIS-style interface: concentric rotating rings, radial ticks, a scanning sweep, state-specific motion) rather than a single pulsing circle, while keeping Direction B/C, no hexagons, and full `prefers-reduced-motion` support. He also asked whether a `/design` or equivalent workflow was available and, if so, to use it first; and, after the animation work, for a complete source-level audit of the whole repository and a reconciliation pass across every governing Markdown file — all still inside the same secondary (non-Mac) devcontainer, with the same standing boundaries (no OAuth/credential/schedule/Hermes changes, no Phase 10B enablement, no Phase 11, no Git operations).

**`/design` workflow check**: a Claude Design canvas-mockup skill is available in this environment, but it publishes a separate static multi-artboard HTML mockup as its own Artifact — it does not edit or critique the live React/CSS animation system in place. Using it would have meant designing a disconnected mockup rather than the actual product, which conflicts with the standing instruction that Phase 6 is implementation work in the real frontend, not a separate design document. It was therefore not invoked; the motion-system design reasoning below was done directly against the real component/CSS code.

**Cinematic `JarvisCore` redesign** (`frontend/src/components/JarvisCore.tsx`, `index.css`): replaced the single-circle-with-a-pulse core with a `.jarvis-hud` wrapper containing five decorative ring/sweep layers built from masked `repeating-conic-gradient`/`conic-gradient` backgrounds (`-webkit-mask`/`mask: radial-gradient(...)` cut a ring shape out of a filled gradient disc) animated purely via `transform: rotate()` keyframes:
- an outer faint boundary (slow opacity/scale "breathe", no rotation),
- a segmented outer ring (rotates clockwise, slowest),
- a radial tick ring (rotates counter-clockwise, a different speed),
- a counter-rotating inner ring (clockwise, faster),
- a scanning conic sweep (clockwise, fastest) —

four independently-animated layers at four different speeds and two different directions, satisfying "at least three ring/arc layers rotate independently at different speeds and directions." A separate pulsing radial-gradient halo and eight small radial "audio bar" elements (opacity 0 except during listening/speaking) round out the state-specific read. Every layer reads `--ring-color`/`--ring-glow`/`--spin-speed` from a single per-`state-*` class block, so tuning or adding a state touches one small rule, not five. The "JARVIS" label and state text live in `.jc-core`, which is never rotated, so they stay upright regardless of the surrounding motion — verified, not just asserted (see below).

**Orbital Home upgrades** (`Home.tsx`): a shared `.orbit-path` ring (one element, not per-node) brightens via `.core-layout:has(.domain-button:hover, .domain-button:focus-visible)` — a modern `:has()` selector, safe here since the only supported runtime is a recent Chromium-based browser (the app-mode window `jarvisctl.sh` opens, and the Chromium this pass tested against). Idle domain nodes get a very slow, low-amplitude "breathing" box-shadow (`node-breathe`, paused on hover/focus/selection so it never fights the existing interactive transition). Clicking a node sets a ~220ms `focusing` flag (scaling/glowing that one node) before calling `onSelectDomain`, reading as "focusing a subsystem" without perceptibly delaying navigation; digit-key navigation intentionally bypasses this and stays instant. `.core-layout` plays a one-shot `home-enter` scale/fade-in on every mount — since Home fully unmounts when a domain is selected and remounts on return, this alone gives "returning home" a coherent reverse-of-selection feel with no extra coordination code.

**Motion strategy**: `transform`/`opacity` and masked gradients only — no animated blur filters, no SVG, no `requestAnimationFrame` loop, bounded DOM (a fixed handful of ring/bar elements, not a particle system). The existing single top-level `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` rule (already zeroing all animation/transition durations globally, from D71) covers every new layer automatically with no new reduced-motion code required.

**Real browser QA in a temporary harness (not a repository dependency)**: Playwright + a downloaded Chromium build were installed under `/tmp/jarvis-visual-qa` (a scratch npm project, its own `package.json`/lockfile — never touching `frontend/package.json` or `frontend/package-lock.json`) and pointed at the actual `vite build` output served via `vite preview`, with `page.route()` intercepting every `/api/**` call with hand-written, clearly-fictional fixture data (no real backend, no real personal data anywhere in a fixture or screenshot). This is real component code and a real (headless) browser, but not the real backend, real data, or real hardware — recorded as such throughout, never as live-Mac acceptance.

This harness *found and fixed two genuine defects* a source-only review had missed:
1. **A page-breaking scroll bug.** D71's CSS had set `overflow-x: hidden` on both `html` and `body` to prevent a horizontal scrollbar. Because `<html>` had an *explicit* (non-`visible`) overflow value, the browser's "propagate `<body>`'s overflow to the viewport" mechanism never engaged, and `<html>`/`<body>`'s pre-existing `height: 100%` (from Phase 1) then clipped the page to exactly one viewport tall — any screen with content taller than that (e.g. a domain view with several expanded memory cards) became **genuinely unreachable by scrolling, with no error and no visual indication**. Confirmed via `window.scrollTo`/wheel-scroll returning `scrollY === 0` against a page with `document.body.scrollHeight` far exceeding `clientHeight`. Fixed by scoping the rule to `body` only (the standard, idiomatic fix — the browser then correctly defers viewport scrolling to `body`'s overflow instead of `html`'s own boxed one), and reverified: a wheel-scroll now moves `scrollY` from 0 to over 1000px on the same screen, with the horizontal-overflow check still passing.
2. **Two unnamed `<select>` elements** (`DomainView.tsx`'s structured-record-type picker and the "Remember" dialog's memory-kind picker) — caught by an `axe-core` scan (`select-name`, the only violation across the whole scan), fixed with `aria-label`. The same missing-label pattern was found and fixed in three more selects discovered by inspection while fixing this (Skills Centre's new-skill domain picker, Memory Centre's new-memory kind picker, Integrations Centre's two automatic-sync-interval pickers and its upload-domain picker) — none had a test asserting a specific accessible name, so none were at risk of a false-negative regression.

After both fixes, a full re-run of the harness (all four viewports) recorded **zero findings**: no horizontal overflow, all six orbit nodes visible and non-overlapping (PATH specifically checked) at every viewport, zero console errors/`pageerror`s, zero axe violations, and every keyboard shortcut (1–6, Cmd+K, Cmd+Shift+E, Escape-cancels-listening, Escape-closes-palette, Space-does-not-fire-while-typing) working through the real rendered UI. Screenshots were genuinely opened and visually inspected (not merely generated) for Home, a domain dashboard, the conversation screen (including the cross-domain sensitivity warning), Memory/Actions/Skills/Integrations/Routine Centres, Data Management, the command palette, and all six voice states plus the reduced-motion variant.

**Rotation was proven, not assumed.** `getComputedStyle(el).transform` was read on `.jc-segmented` and `.jc-ticks` roughly a second apart under normal motion — both changed, in the two different expected directions (CW/CCW), matching the configured revolution durations to within measurement error — then the identical read was repeated under Playwright's `reducedMotion: 'reduce'` emulation and returned the literal string `"none"` at both reads, confirming rotation genuinely stops rather than merely running an imperceptibly slow variant.

**A minor, unrelated top-bar UX gap found during this pass**: at narrow widths the top bar's centre-navigation buttons scroll internally (by design, to avoid a page-level horizontal scrollbar) but previously gave no visual hint that more items existed off-screen. Fixed with a `ResizeObserver` (`App.tsx`) toggling `.top-bar.is-scrollable` only when the bar is actually wider than its viewport, which applies a trailing-edge `mask-image` fade — conditional on real overflow state, not a permanent fade that would incorrectly dim a fully-visible last item at normal widths. Guarded for the test environment (`typeof ResizeObserver === "undefined"`), since jsdom has no `ResizeObserver`.

**Isolated backend verification** (`backend/.venv` — the real, macOS-built virtualenv — deliberately untouched): a fresh Python 3.14 virtualenv was created under `/tmp/jarvis-backend-venv` and populated from `backend/pyproject.toml`'s dependency list (`faster-whisper`/`edge-tts` were not needed — `app.main` and the full test suite, including `test_voice.py`, import and pass without them, confirming those heavy ML dependencies are only ever imported lazily inside actual transcription/speech code paths, not at module load). **418/418 backend pytest tests pass**, including a new focused regression test (`test_callback_error_query_param_is_html_escaped_not_injected` in `tests/test_integrations_api.py`) added specifically to cover D71's `html.escape()` fix — it asserts that a `<script>`/`<img onerror>` payload passed as the OAuth callback's `error` query parameter appears in the response only as inert, escaped text (`&lt;script&gt;`), never as a live tag.

**Full-repository audit findings** (source-level only; no real Mac/Keychain/Google/Hermes/microphone access; a targeted grep-driven sweep, not a literal line-by-line read of every file): no `TODO`/`FIXME`/`HACK` anywhere in `backend/app` or `frontend/src`; no stale Claude/Anthropic assumptions or hardcoded model strings in source (model identity only ever comes from `GET /api/agent/status`, matching D71's provider-independence claim); no code-level "sub-agent" reference that wasn't an explicit docstring *denial* ("never a sub-agent" — `scheduler_runtime.py`, `scheduler_service.py`, `models_scheduler.py`, reinforcing the standing exclusion rather than violating it); every "Fitbit" mention in source is correctly framed as one of several possible Google Health data sources, matching D64's correction, with one exception: a stale docstring comment in `backend/app/routers/integrations.py` still referenced an old, no-longer-used UI phrase ("Fitbit via Google Health") — corrected to describe the actual current UI copy and cite D64 directly. No secrets, tokens, confirmation tokens, or raw OAuth payloads found rendered anywhere in the frontend (re-confirmed by grep, consistent with D71). No new backend route, schema, or migration was touched by any part of this pass beyond the one-line callback-escaping fix from D71.

**Documentation reconciliation**: `README.md` gained a "What Phase 6 includes" section (previously it still described Phase 6 as wholly future work in its intro paragraph and its "explicitly out of scope" list) and had its Phase 9 scope-item wording corrected to stop listing Calendar/Health integrations as "out of scope" now that they're long complete; `docs/PRODUCT_SPEC.md` §4 was corrected the same way. `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §9f was rewritten to describe the actual ring-layer implementation, the Home motion additions, and the container-QA-vs-real-Mac boundary precisely (rather than the earlier single-pulse description). `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 6 entry now describes the cinematic HUD, the container browser QA and what it found/fixed, and the isolated-backend full-suite result, while still explicitly not marking real-Mac acceptance complete. `CLAUDE.md` and `docs/DESIGN_DIRECTION.md` were reviewed and needed no changes — nothing in this pass altered a standing product rule they document.

**Cross-document contradiction check**: HUD/voice remain core (not optional) everywhere they're mentioned; six domains are consistently "context spaces," never "agents"; memory is consistently Jarvis's local database, never Hermes/the model's; storage is consistently local-first (`JARVIS_DATA_DIR`), never cloud; voice is consistently push-to-talk, never always-listening; Google Health is consistently read-only; Calendar writes consistently require Phase 8 approval; Hermes toolsets are consistently described as disabled (D68/D69/D70's addendum, unchanged and untouched by this pass); sub-agents are consistently a deliberate V1 exclusion, not planned work; routines are consistently "implemented, not yet enabled" (Phase 10B still deferred, untouched here); Phase 6 is consistently "implementation and container QA complete, real-Mac acceptance pending" across README/ARCHITECTURE/ROADMAP/DECISIONS after this pass's edits; Phase 11 is consistently not started; Git is consistently not to be used without an explicit per-turn request. No contradiction found requiring further correction.

**Why**: Bernardo's instruction was to keep the cinematic upgrade honest about what a devcontainer can and cannot prove — richer motion is a real product requirement, but "richer" must still mean "verified to actually animate, actually stop under reduced motion, and actually reachable/scrollable," not just "more CSS keyframes exist." The scroll bug in particular would have been a serious, silent regression (content unreachable, no error) had it shipped past a source-only review; this is the concrete argument for why the temporary browser harness was worth building.
**How to apply**: see `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 6 entry for the exact remaining real-Mac steps (real browser + real data visual confirmation at the same viewports, one real push-to-talk voice-state walkthrough, and a reconfirmation that the authenticated `/v1/toolsets` endpoint still reports zero enabled toolsets, since no Hermes configuration was touched but it's cheap to recheck).

## D73 — Correction: Phase 10B's routine schedule values were already selected, not still awaiting selection

**Context**: Bernardo gave his exact Phase 10B routine schedule selections (Morning Briefing, Evening Check-in, Weekly Review — see below) in a message earlier in the same overall working relationship, well before the Phase 6 work described in D71/D72. The final report delivered at the end of the D72 pass nonetheless described Phase 10B as "still awaiting your explicit real-Mac schedule values," which Bernardo correctly flagged as inaccurate — the values were never in question; only *applying* them has been blocked, every time, by whichever session was working not having access to the real Mac's `~/JarvisData`/backend.

**Decision**: record the exact selections here as the durable source of truth, and correct every document that could be read as saying the values are still undecided.

* **Morning Briefing**: enabled, daily, `08:00`, timezone `Europe/London`, include BODY, exclude MIND, exclude PEOPLE (LIFE/PATH/BUILD remain the always-included baseline, unaffected by domain selection).
* **Evening Check-in**: enabled, daily, `21:30`, timezone `Europe/London`.
* **Weekly Review**: enabled, weekday Sunday, `18:00`, timezone `Europe/London`, all six domains included (BODY, MIND, PEOPLE, PATH, BUILD, LIFE).

**Corrected**: `README.md` (the Phase 10B section's closing bullet), `docs/ROADMAP.md` (the Phase 10 header line and the Phase 10B Status paragraph's "Not yet done" sentence), `docs/DECISIONS.md` D70's "How to apply" line (this file). No implementation change was made alongside this correction — no schedule was enabled, no database was created or touched, and this remains a documentation-only fix.
**Why**: an inaccurate status report is worse than no report — a future session (or Bernardo himself) reading "still awaiting selection" would either re-ask a question already answered or, worse, assume a decision needs to be re-made. The values only ever need to be entered once; what's actually blocking activation is environmental (real-Mac access), not decisional.
**How to apply**: the next session with genuine access to the real Jarvis instance should issue the three `PUT /api/routines/{routine_type}/schedule` calls with the exact values above, verify each returned `next_due_at`, and only then mark this line of Phase 10B complete.

## D74 — Home/shell refined past "generic futuristic dashboard," plus a deterministic local UI-command layer

**Context**: after D71/D72's cinematic HUD pass, Bernardo's review was that the result, while technically solid, still read as a generic AI-generated futuristic dashboard rather than a deliberately designed product — specifically: domain nodes crowded with full paragraph descriptions, six identical rectangular "Centre" pill buttons in the header, inconsistent/generic hover-transition treatment, and no way for Jarvis to understand a spoken or typed interface command (e.g. "open Integrations Centre") without sending it to a model. This was requested and implemented in the same secondary devcontainer, against the same live interactive preview (D-series container-preview boundary unchanged), with the same standing constraints (no backend/schema/OAuth changes, no routine enablement, no Phase 11, no Git operations).

**Orbital node simplification**: `DomainNode.tsx` was rewritten to show only the domain name and a short, fixed, hand-written 2-3 concept subtitle (`frontend/src/domainSubtitles.ts` — e.g. BODY: "Health · Training · Recovery") — never the full API-supplied `description` field, which previously filled the circle with a full sentence. That full, truthful description (still sourced directly from `GET /api/domains`, never rewritten) now appears only in a new `DomainInfoPanel.tsx`, shown for whichever node is currently hovered or keyboard-focused (`Home.tsx` tracks `hoveredSlug`) and animating in/out (`domain-info-enter`, ~240ms fade+translate) rather than being permanently visible. Idle nodes lost their periodic "breathing" box-shadow animation entirely — a resting node is now genuinely static (1px `--border-subtle` border, `--shadow-sm` only) until actually hovered/focused, at which point it gets a cyan border, a small `translateY(-1px) scale(1.04)` lift, a short inward "connector" stub (`.domain-connector`, oriented via the node's own placement angle) fading in, and the shared `.orbit-path` ring brightening via a `:has(.domain-button:hover, .domain-button:focus-visible)` selector. The 1-6 keyboard-shortcut numbers moved from bare floating text into small bordered circular badges (`.domain-kbd`) that pick up the same cyan treatment on hover, rather than looking like an accidental leftover overlay.

**Shell consolidation**: the six identical outlined "Centre" buttons in the top bar were replaced with one `SystemsMenu.tsx` trigger ("Systems", with a small square icon) that opens a compact popover listing the same six destinations as menu items — closes on Escape, on an outside click, and on selecting an item; every previously-reachable route remains exactly one interaction away. A separate small `⌘K`-labeled button sits beside it so the command palette's existence stays visually obvious without the header needing six equal-weight rectangles to do it. The header now reads left-to-right as brand/breadcrumb → status → compact controls, rather than a row of generic SaaS-dashboard pills.

**Motion system**: introduced exactly two new duration tokens (`--motion-control: 190ms`, `--motion-panel: 240ms`) and one shared easing curve (`--ease-hud: cubic-bezier(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1)`), applied consistently to every button, nav item, domain node, card action, and command-palette row's hover/active/focus transition, and to every panel's entrance (command palette, Systems menu, domain info panel). Hover consistently uses a targeted `border-color`/`background`/`box-shadow` transition plus `translateY(-1px)` (never `transition: all` — confirmed absent by grep); active/pressed consistently uses `scale(0.98)`; disabled controls explicitly force `transform: none` and `box-shadow: none` so a disabled button never appears to lift or glow on hover. `prefers-reduced-motion`'s existing single global rule (D71/D72) required no changes to cover any of this new motion.

**Deterministic local UI-command layer** (`frontend/src/commands/registry.ts`, `highlight.ts`): a small, pure, fully unit-tested (52 cases, `registry.test.ts`) command parser shared by the Command Palette (`resolveCommand` prop, typed input) and `DomainView`'s voice transcript handling (spoken input, checked before a transcript is ever sent to Jarvis as a conversation turn). It never calls Hermes or any model — matching is exact-string-after-verb-stripping against fixed alias tables (deliberately not substring/"contains" matching, since that would risk misreading an ordinary conversational sentence ending in a domain word, e.g. "...affecting my body," as a navigation command). Four outcomes: `navigate` (safe, executes immediately — the six centres with aliases like "integrations"/"routines", the six domains with aliases like "health area"/"career space", home, back, command palette), `focus_control` (a phrase naming one of the specific sensitive Integrations-Centre controls named in Bernardo's brief — Calendar/Health connect, disconnect, enable-writing, automatic-sync, matched via required-keywords-in-any-order so both "connect google calendar" and "disconnect google health" resolve correctly regardless of word order — a real bug caught by the new tests, since the first implementation's regexes assumed a fixed word order and silently misclassified the reversed phrasing as `blocked`), `blocked` (a mutating/destructive verb combined with a recognizable system noun, or a positional-selection phrase like "press the second button" — refused with a spoken/displayed explanation, never executed), or `none` (falls through unchanged to existing behavior — the palette's static action list, or DomainView's normal send-to-Jarvis flow). `focus_control` results only navigate to Integrations Centre and apply a temporary `.command-highlight` pulse to the exact `data-command-target`-tagged element (via a bounded `setInterval` poll, since the target component may still be fetching its own data when navigation happens) — they never click, check, or submit anything; the UI text explicitly says "shows, does not activate."

**Known scope boundary, stated rather than silently omitted**: voice commands "outside a domain" (i.e. with no active conversation to fall back to) are not reachable in the live product yet, because no push-to-talk control exists on the Home screen — voice remains scoped to an active `DomainView` conversation, unchanged since Phase 5. The parser's contract for that case (return `none` rather than guessing) is implemented and tested as a pure function, but adding a Home-level microphone entry point was judged a distinct new feature beyond "add a command layer to the existing surfaces" and was not built in this pass.

**Why**: the header/node changes are a legibility and restraint argument — Bernardo's own framing was that six identical pills and paragraph-filled circles read as generated rather than designed, and the fix in both cases is the same principle (show only what's needed at rest, reveal detail on deliberate interaction). The command layer's exact-match-only parsing and keyword-order-independent sensitive-control matching are both direct responses to concrete false-positive/false-negative bugs the new test suite caught during this same pass (a "contains" match on domain aliases would have misrouted real BODY/MIND conversation; the original ordered sensitive-control regex silently misclassified "disconnect google health" as a generic blocked phrase instead of a specific highlightable control) — recorded here so a future session doesn't reintroduce either by "simplifying" the matching logic.
**How to apply**: `frontend/src/commands/registry.test.ts` is the executable specification for what the parser does and doesn't recognize — extend it first when adding a new alias or control, and keep exact-match/keyword-order-independence as the default posture for any new entry rather than reaching for substring matching. The Home-level voice entry point remains a legitimate, currently-unbuilt follow-up if Bernardo wants "outside a domain" voice commands to actually be reachable.

## D75 — The Jarvis core becomes a general-conversation entry point (a scope, not a seventh domain), a refined hover/rotation motion system, and a shared internal-console design language replacing the generic-admin-dashboard look

**Context**: three sequential Phase 6 requests, the third amended mid-pass. (1) Make the central Jarvis core a real interactive control that opens a general conversation — explicitly not a seventh domain. (2) Retune hover/rotation motion to feel less reactive, with specific timing ranges. (3) Originally "polish internal pages into a premium interface," redirected mid-task once Bernardo reviewed the in-progress result and judged it "a themed React admin dashboard" — large rounded purple cards stacked vertically, every page sharing one composition — with an explicit instruction not to just add more glow/rotating circles, but to build a coherent console design language derived from Home and give each page a layout suited to its purpose. Same secondary-devcontainer boundary as D71-D74 (no OAuth/credential changes, no routine enablement, no Phase 11, no Git operations, no real model call).

**General conversation — schema and persistence**: `conversations.domain_id` and `context_snapshots.active_domain_id` became nullable (migration `0011_general_conversations.py`, forward-only after `0010`). NULL means "general conversation" — the `domains` table is untouched and still seeds exactly six rows; nothing fabricates or reuses a "JARVIS" domain. A new `backend/app/routers/general.py` exposes `POST`/`GET /api/general/conversations` (domain_id always NULL); the existing domain-agnostic `/api/conversations/{id}/messages` and `/api/conversations/{id}/turns` endpoints are reused unchanged for both kinds of conversation. `context_builder.build_context` takes `domain: Domain | None`; when `None`, every domain-scoped section (summary/memories/records, and their `in_scope_domain_ids`/`in_scope_slugs` contributions) is skipped entirely — a general turn's only route to domain material is the pre-existing `additional_domain_ids` per-turn mechanism, exactly as DomainView already used it for cross-domain inclusion. Because export/backup/restore already copy the whole SQLite file and Hermes profile wholesale, general conversations required no additional export-service code — proven by two new tests that create a general conversation, run a real export/restore round-trip, and assert it survives with `domain_id` still NULL and the domain count still six.
