**A subtle SQLite migration hazard, caught before it touched data**: the first draft of migration 0011 batch-altered `conversations` (and `context_snapshots`) without disabling `PRAGMA foreign_keys` first. `app/database.py` forces `foreign_keys=ON` on every connection, including Alembic's own — so SQLite's batch-mode table recreation (create new, copy rows, **drop old**) silently cascade-deleted every `messages`/`agent_runs` row referencing the old `conversations` table, and transitively every `context_snapshots` row referencing those. Caught immediately by running the full existing backend suite after writing the migration (`test_migration_0002`/`0003`'s data-preservation tests failed with rows unexpectedly `None`) — before any test asserting new behavior was even written. Fixed by wrapping both `upgrade()` and `downgrade()`'s batch operations in explicit `PRAGMA foreign_keys=OFF` / `...=ON` (try/finally); full suite re-run clean. Recorded here because this is a subtle, easily-reintroduced class of bug for any future migration that batch-alters a table other tables reference with `ON DELETE CASCADE` — the toggle must not be dropped as unnecessary boilerplate.

**General conversation — frontend and privacy behaviour**: `JarvisCore.tsx` gained an optional `onActivate`/`activating` pair; when supplied (Home only — every other use of the component stays the unchanged, non-interactive `role="status"` indicator), it renders as a real `<button>` with accessible name "Talk to Jarvis," a restrained hover/focus response (`scale(1.015)` plus a local drop-shadow, no ring-animation changes), and a brief 260ms "energize" delay before the route actually changes — the same pattern `DomainNode`'s `FOCUS_TRANSITION_MS` already used for domain selection, now shared conceptually rather than literally shared code. `views/GeneralConversation.tsx` is a new, separate view (not DomainView with a domain stripped out): it has no domain summary/memories/records rail, because a general turn never auto-retrieves any domain-scoped material by default. Its context rail shows exactly six per-turn domain checkboxes (`SENSITIVE_SLUGS`, now extracted to `frontend/src/sensitiveDomains.ts` and shared with `DomainView.tsx` rather than duplicated) with the same BODY/MIND/PEOPLE acknowledgement-required warning; a selection applies to that one turn only and is cleared after send, proven by a frontend test that sends two turns back-to-back and asserts the second's `additional_domain_ids` is empty. "Remember" calls `createMemory({ scope: "global", ... })` with no `domain_id` key at all (asserted directly in `GeneralConversation.test.tsx`), never a domain-assigned memory. New backend tests (`test_general_conversations.py`, 9) assert: exactly six domains exist; a general conversation's `domain_id` is `None`; a default turn's context snapshot has empty `domain_memory_version_ids`/`domain_summary_version_ids`/`structured_record_ids` and a non-empty global list; explicit per-turn inclusion works and does not persist to the next turn; domain isolation between a real domain conversation and general is unaffected; and exactly one `FakeProvider` call is made (no real model contact). New frontend tests (`App.test.tsx`, `GeneralConversation.test.tsx`) cover the click/keyboard entry point, the six-chip default-unchecked state, the sensitivity warning, and the Remember scope.

**Motion refinement**: a genuine hover-intent delay (100ms, `HOVER_INTENT_MS` in `Home.tsx`) now gates a domain node's hover ring behind a real `setTimeout`, cancelled on pointer-leave before it fires — so a pointer sweeping across several nodes on its way elsewhere never lights any of them, while keyboard focus (`DomainNode`'s `onFocus`, a separate prop from `onPointerEnter`) bypasses the delay entirely, since a keyboard user is never "passing through." Ring entrance/exit got their own durations (`--motion-ring-enter: 300ms`, `--motion-ring-exit: 200ms`) instead of sharing one 190ms control duration, so appearing reads calmer than disappearing. Rotation speeds were retimed rather than redesigned: node hover rings 9s/6s → 10s/14s (inner/outer); node "is-focusing" (selected/command-highlighted) rings 20s/14s → 27s/19s; JarvisCore's fastest ambient layer (`.jc-spin-cw-fast`) 16s → 20s and its halo pulse 2.4s → 3s (both ~25% slower — the two layers that read as most "frantic" at idle); the scanning sweep only 4.2s → 4.8s, deliberately kept the fastest ambient layer per Bernardo's explicit allowance. No `transition: all`, no JS animation loop, no `requestAnimationFrame` were introduced; the existing single global `prefers-reduced-motion` rule covers all of this with no new code.

**Internal-console design system** (`frontend/src/components/console/Console.tsx`): eight small presentational primitives — `ConsolePage` (implicit, via the `.console-canvas` class `App.tsx` applies to `.app-main` whenever off Home), `ConsoleHeader`, `ConsoleModule`, `ConsoleSectionLabel`, `TelemetryRow`, `MiniCoreIndicator`, `ContextRail`, `TechnicalDetails`. The shared visual language lives mostly in `index.css` as a rewrite of the *existing* shared selectors (`.domain-header`, the blanket `section[aria-label]` rule that every Centre already inherited) rather than new one-off classes, so every Centre picked up the reskin automatically: `--bg-panel-sunken` matte surfaces, `--radius-md` (10px, down from 16px), 1px `--border-subtle` borders (no more `linear-gradient`/radial-blob "hero card" background), and a new near-invisible fixed grid texture (`.console-canvas`, two 1px-line `linear-gradient`s at 42px spacing, ~5% opacity) standing in for Home's atmospheric violet glow specifically on internal pages — Home's own background is untouched. `.console-module.is-live` is the one place cyan appears on a module border, reserved for genuinely active/executing state, never decorative. `MiniCoreIndicator`'s one ring segment only rotates when its `active` prop is true, driven by real state (`sendingToJarvis || voiceState !== "idle"` in DomainView/GeneralConversation; `busy` in IntegrationsCentre) — never a permanent per-card decoration.

**Domain view rebuilt as a "cockpit"**: `DomainView.tsx`'s old `.domain-grid` (identity/memory/records column beside conversation) became `.cockpit-layout` — a ~68%/32% grid (`cockpit-main`/`cockpit-rail`, stacking below 1100px) putting the conversation itself in the wider primary column and summary/memories/records/cross-domain-selector into a `ContextRail` of `ConsoleModule`s on the side, closer to how much of the page each is actually used. The header became a `ConsoleHeader` with a `MiniCoreIndicator` and a truthful conversation/memory count (shown only when the API actually returned nonzero counts, never a fabricated zero). The persistent dashed "Save as note vs. Send to Jarvis" explanation banner moved behind a `TechnicalDetails` disclosure. An explicit empty state ("Start a conversation, or open one from the list above.") replaced silently rendering nothing when no conversation is selected. `GeneralConversation.tsx` was built directly against this same cockpit/module language from the start. Every existing accessible name, placeholder, and button label DomainView's tests depend on (`Send to Jarvis`, `Save as note`, `Remember`, `Confirm remember`, `Context used`, `Hold to talk`, the sensitive-domain checkbox text) was preserved exactly — all 8 `DomainView.test.tsx` and 4 `DomainView.voice.test.tsx` cases pass unchanged, confirming the restructuring is presentational.

**Centres — graduated depth, reported honestly rather than silently uneven**: all six Centres (Memory, Actions, Skills, Integrations, Routine, Data Management) got the shared reskin automatically (matte modules, thin borders, grid canvas) plus a consistent `ConsoleHeader`/`MiniCoreIndicator`/"Centre" eyebrow replacing their old plain `<h1>`/`<p>` header, and Integrations Centre's two raw OAuth-scope lines moved behind `TechnicalDetails` disclosures. Beyond that shared treatment, only Actions Centre and Skills Centre already had — from earlier phases, not from this pass — structure that substantially matches their described identity (Actions: a pending-proposal queue with reason/source/effect visible and distinct Approve/Deny; Skills: draft/active/archived lifecycle tabs). Memory, Integrations, Routine, and Data Management did **not** get a full bespoke module-arrangement rebuild in this pass beyond the shared header/surface treatment — this is a real scope boundary, not an oversight, and is called out explicitly in the same-session final report rather than claimed as complete.

**Why**: the general-conversation privacy default (global-only, explicit-and-single-turn domain inclusion) mirrors the exact reasoning CLAUDE.md §2 already applies to domain isolation — a system prompt for "no specific domain" must not become a backdoor that silently sees everything, so it defaults to seeing the least, not the most. The console redesign's specific complaint (identical purple-gradient cards everywhere) traced directly to two blanket CSS rules doing double duty as both "shared visual language" and "the only layout every page gets" — fixing the rules' *colors/borders* reached every page at once, but fixing the *composition* required page-specific work that only DomainView (the explicitly named "strongest structural change") and the newly-built GeneralConversation received in full this pass.
**How to apply**: `backend/app/context_builder.py`'s docstring is the authoritative statement of how a general turn's context is assembled — read it before adding any new context source, and route any new source through `additional_domain_ids` if it should ever be optionally includable from a general conversation. `frontend/src/components/console/Console.tsx`'s primitives are the intended building blocks for any further internal-page work; a future pass giving Memory/Integrations/Routine/Data Management their own described bespoke layout (ledger, connected-systems console, schedule deck, local vault) should start from `ConsoleModule`/`ContextRail`/`TelemetryRow` rather than reintroducing ad hoc card markup.

## D76 — Permanent frontend-aesthetics doctrine adopted, adapted from the Claude Cookbook's "Prompting for frontend aesthetics" methodology

**Context**: D75's correction (internal pages reading as "a themed React admin dashboard") was a concrete, lived instance of a general failure mode — visual work regressing toward generic AI/SaaS defaults (rounded cards, gradient heroes, evenly-distributed accent colors, glassmorphism) whenever it isn't anchored to something product-specific. Bernardo asked for this to be addressed permanently rather than corrected case-by-case: a durable doctrine in `CLAUDE.md` itself, expanded from an earlier, shorter `<jarvis_frontend_aesthetics>` block already present there, adapted specifically to Jarvis rather than copied from the Cookbook article's own examples. This was documentation-and-guidance work only — no component was redesigned, no application behavior changed, and no other broad visual pass was started in this same session turn, per Bernardo's explicit boundary.

**What changed**: `CLAUDE.md`'s existing `<jarvis_frontend_aesthetics>` section (Visual identity/Motion/Internal screens/Avoid/one evaluation line) was replaced in place with a more complete version covering the same tag, now with explicit subsections — Context-specific identity, Typography, Motion, Background and atmosphere, Internal screens, Interaction quality, Evaluation — each stated in terms already established elsewhere in this project (Direction B/C, the six domains, the existing color semantics, the existing approval lifecycle) rather than generic design advice. `docs/DESIGN_DIRECTION.md` gained a new section, "Why Jarvis needs its own visual grammar, not generic AI/SaaS styling," explaining: why the generic-AI-slop failure mode happens; why a context-specific grammar counters it; the distinction between cinematic states (Home/core/voice/full-page faults) and calmer operational screens (domain conversations, the general conversation, the Centres); the color semantics (violet/cyan/amber/red); the motion hierarchy and the hover-intent-delay rationale; why repeating identical cards is banned as a default solution (citing D75 as the concrete example); why visual work must be judged from real screenshots/interaction states rather than source inspection alone; and an explicit statement that the Cookbook article informs prompting *methodology* here, not this project's actual visual template.

**Why**: a doctrine stated once, permanently, in the file every session is required to read before touching the frontend (`CLAUDE.md` itself) is far more durable than relying on each session's own judgment or on repeating the same correction after each visual regression. Explaining *why* the rules exist (in `docs/DESIGN_DIRECTION.md`, alongside the shorter imperative rules in `CLAUDE.md`) matters because a future session that only sees "never repeat identical cards" as a bare rule has no way to judge a genuinely novel edge case the rule didn't anticipate; a session that also has the reasoning (identical cards read as generic *because* the information they hold differs and deserves a different structure) can extend the doctrine correctly rather than pattern-matching narrowly on the letter of the rule.

**How to apply**: read `CLAUDE.md`'s `<jarvis_frontend_aesthetics>` section before any frontend visual work, the same way the rest of `CLAUDE.md` is required reading before modifying the project. Use `docs/DESIGN_DIRECTION.md`'s new section when the *reasoning* behind a rule matters — e.g. deciding whether a genuinely new UI pattern counts as "cinematic" or "operational," or whether a proposed color use fits the existing violet/cyan/amber/red semantics. Do not add a second, competing copy of this doctrine elsewhere; if it needs to change, edit `CLAUDE.md`'s block in place and record the change here, the same append-only way as this entry.

## D77 — A truthful diagnostic-fault system replacing a single generic error treatment: 404, controller offline, Hermes degraded, an interface crash, module-level failures, and the OAuth callback page all read as distinct, real states

**Context**: the prior "backend unavailable" experience was a small red `JarvisCore` inside a visible square with a floating badge and no recovery action — one generic treatment reused for every kind of fault, with no way to tell an unreachable controller from a degraded model link from a plain 404. This pass (still inside the same secondary, non-Mac devcontainer, same standing boundaries — no OAuth/credential/schedule/Hermes changes, no routine enablement, no Phase 11, no Git operations, no real model call) built a shared diagnostic-fault system and gave each real fault condition its own truthful state, explicitly never reusing one screen for conditions that are actually different.

**Shared system** (`frontend/src/components/diagnostic/Diagnostic.tsx`): `DiagnosticCore`/`DiagnosticCode`/`DiagnosticPage`/`RecoveryActions`/`ModuleErrorState`, plus `ErrorBoundary.tsx`, `NotFoundDiagnostic.tsx`, `ControllerOfflineDiagnostic.tsx`, and `ModelLinkBanner.tsx`. Color is load-bearing and enforced by a fixed `DiagnosticTone` union (`critical`/`degraded`/`neutral`/`recovered`) — red only for a genuine unavailable/failure state, amber for degraded-but-usable, violet/cyan for a navigation condition, never decorative. Every ring is deliberately incomplete rather than a calmer version of the healthy core: `DiagnosticRingVariant` is `gap` (a static ring with one missing segment — 404, since the system itself isn't broken), `interrupted` (a stutter-once rotation with a connector line that fades before reaching the core — the terminal, non-retrying interface-crash state), `dual` (a healthy outer ring, only the inner ring gapped and static — Hermes degraded while the backend stays up), or `reconnecting` (Controller Offline's own variant: smooth continuous outer rotation, counter-rotating inner trace, a breathing core, a probe that extends/retracts as if attempting a handshake, all visibly accelerating during a real retry attempt).

**Truthful distinctions, not one generic "error" state**:
- **Unavailable** (`ControllerOfflineDiagnostic`) — the local controller itself isn't answering. Explanation text is explicit that locally stored data "has not been deleted," since an outage must never be allowed to read as data loss. No URL is hardcoded in the copy (the controller base URL is configurable — see `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`).
- **Disconnected** — reserved for an actual OAuth/integration provider connection state (`IntegrationConnection.status`, Phase 9/10), never conflated with the controller being unreachable; a status-check failure there was already required (D66) to surface as `"error"`, never silently reinterpreted as `"disconnected"`, and this pass didn't relax that.
- **Unknown** — `ModuleErrorState` exists specifically so a single failed supplementary request (e.g. Google Health summaries failing while connection status still loaded fine) renders "status is unknown, not empty" rather than silently falling back to an empty list that would read as "zero records exist." Applied for real in `IntegrationsCentre.tsx` for Health summaries/sessions, with a regression test proving one provider's failure can't demote an unrelated, successfully-fetched provider's status.
- **Failed** — a genuine attempt that ran and did not succeed (a turn error, an OAuth exchange failure, a module fetch that threw) — distinct from "unknown" (never attempted/status not yet known) and from "unavailable" (nothing to even attempt against).
- **Degraded** (`ModelLinkBanner`, amber, `variant="dual"`) — Hermes/the model gateway is unreachable while the local controller stays up. Deliberately never a full-page takeover: the banner explicitly states local notes/memories/records/saved data remain available, offers "Retry model connection," "Continue without model" (Home), and "Save as note" (composer contexts) — never disables local-only functionality just because Hermes is down.
- **Navigation fault, not a system fault** (`NotFoundDiagnostic`, tone `neutral`) — an unrecognized route. This app has exactly one real frontend route (`/`); `App.tsx` computes `isKnownRoute` once from `window.location.pathname` at mount and renders this instead of silently pretending to be Home for anything else.

**Backend/API 404 vs. frontend route handling** (`backend/app/main.py`): the pre-existing `/api/{full_path:path}` catch-all (registered first, all methods) still returns a genuine 404 for any unmatched `/api/*` path — untouched by this pass. A new, separate SPA fallback is registered after it: `/assets` is mounted as its own `StaticFiles` (`check_dir=False`, so a fixture/test dist directory without a real `assets/` folder doesn't crash `StaticFiles`'s own startup check), and a final `GET /{full_path:path}` route serves a real root-level static file verbatim when one exists (favicon, etc.; path-traversal-safe — resolved path must stay under `FRONTEND_DIST_DIR`) or falls back to `index.html` (200, not a bare framework 404) for anything else, so the SPA can mount and `NotFoundDiagnostic` can render truthfully instead of the browser showing an unstyled error page. Because the `/api/*` catch-all is registered first and matches every method, it is reached before this fallback for any `/api/*` path regardless — no ordering hazard. Three new backend tests cover: the fallback serving index.html for an unknown path, a real root-level file being served verbatim, and the path-traversal guard.

**No fabricated connection state, anywhere**: `ControllerOfflineDiagnostic`'s retry status only ever shows `"Retrying in Ns…"` or `"Attempting reconnection…"` while a request is genuinely scheduled/in-flight, and the `"CONTROLLER LINK // RESTORED"` label and color transition (red → violet → cyan, driven by real `DiagnosticTone` swaps consumed by transitioned `border-color`/`box-shadow` properties, not a custom-property transition that browsers don't reliably interpolate) only ever play after `checkHealth()` has genuinely resolved `true` — proven by a test that asserts `onRecovered` has *not* been called yet at the moment the RESTORED label first appears. `ModuleErrorState` never claims "0" or "no records" for a request that failed rather than genuinely returning empty.

**Automatic bounded retry and manual retry**: `RETRY_DELAYS_MS = [5000, 10000, 20000, 30000]` — bounded (never keeps escalating faster, never shorter), and the App-level 10s health-poll interval explicitly pauses itself while `health === "error"` (`useEffect` gated on `health`) so the two retry loops never compete for the same backend. Retry stops entirely while `document.hidden` (tab not visible) and resumes on `visibilitychange`. Manual retry (`handleManualRetry`) always works regardless of the countdown's current state, immediately cancelling any pending scheduled attempt first.

**Launch instructions**: a single `<details>`/`<summary>` disclosure (native expanded-state semantics, one control, one chevron rotated via the `[open]` attribute) shows the generic `jarvisctl.sh open` command in a copyable block — never an absolute path, since the existing launcher can be referenced generically. An earlier version of this had a genuine bug (a separate "Show/Hide launch instructions" button *around* the disclosure, producing two controls for one piece of state) that was found and fixed in the same pass a follow-up review caught it.

**Diagnostic orbital motion and reduced-motion behaviour**: `variant="reconnecting"`'s idle motion (outer ring ~14s/rev clockwise, inner trace ~9.5s/rev counter-clockwise, a ~3.2s breathing core glow, a ~2.6s probe extend/retract) and its retry-state acceleration (~2.5s/rev, one brighter scan pulse, the probe reaching further toward the centre) were verified as genuine motion — not just screenshotted — by reading the outer/inner rings' real computed `transform` matrices twice ~700-1000ms apart in an actual Chromium browser (Playwright, not jsdom, which has no CSS animation timeline at all) and confirming the angle changed, confirming the two rings rotate in measurably opposite directions, and confirming the retry-state angular delta is measurably larger than the idle one. The existing single global `prefers-reduced-motion` rule (D71/D72, zeroing every `animation-duration`/`transition-duration`) was re-confirmed to cover all of this new motion with no additional code — verified by reading the same transform twice ~800ms apart under `reducedMotion: "reduce"` and confirming it did not change at all.

**OAuth callback page** (`backend/app/routers/integrations.py`): `_callback_page()` was rebuilt to share the same diagnostic visual language (a small inline-SVG segmented ring, gapped for failure/whole for success, cyan for success/red for failure) using plain Python constants for the token *values* (never importing frontend CSS, since this page is rendered by FastAPI, not bundled by Vite) — provider name and a sanitized reason are shown, `html.escape()` is still applied to every externally-supplied or exception-derived value (pre-existing regression test unaffected), OAuth codes/state/tokens are never rendered (new regression test asserts this directly), "Return to Integrations Centre" links to `/?open=integrations` (a genuine deep link — `App.tsx` reads `?open=` once on mount and opens the real centre, then strips the query string), and "Try connection again" is offered only for the cancelled case (a safe, sensible retry), never for a genuine exchange failure where blindly retrying isn't a distinct safe action.

**Why**: a single generic error screen actively hides the one piece of information a person needs most in a fault — what's actually different this time. Conflating "the backend is down" with "the model is down" would make Bernardo think all of Jarvis is unusable when his local data and notes are still fully available; conflating "nothing has loaded yet" with "this genuinely failed" would make a transient module hiccup look like permanent data loss. Distinguishing these costs a handful of extra states, not a fundamentally larger system, since they all share one visual language and one small primitive set.
**How to apply**: `frontend/src/components/diagnostic/Diagnostic.tsx`'s doc comment on `DiagnosticTone`/`DiagnosticRingVariant` is the authoritative statement of what each combination means — reach for an existing tone/variant pairing before inventing a new one, and never reuse `tone="critical"` for a condition that isn't a genuine unavailable/failure state. Any new supplementary/module-level fetch should route through `ModuleErrorState` rather than letting a failed request silently masquerade as an empty list.

## D78 — A real Web Audio waveform for listening and speaking, replacing the generic voice animation, with three genuine bugs found and fixed along the way

**Context**: the push-to-talk listening/speaking states previously used only `JarvisCore`'s existing decorative ring states — no visual connection to the actual microphone input or the actual synthesized speech being played. This pass (same devcontainer/boundaries as D77; no real model call, fictional/mock audio fixtures only) built a genuinely audio-reactive waveform for both directions and wired it into the two real push-to-talk surfaces that exist today.

**`useAudioLevels.ts`** — one shared Web Audio analyser hook for two genuine sources, never a randomized/decorative substitute: `{ kind: "stream", stream }` (the exact `MediaStream` push-to-talk already acquired via `getUserMedia` — never requests permission again, and the analyser tap is never connected onward, so the microphone is never routed to speakers) and `{ kind: "element", element }` (the exact `<audio>` element already playing synthesized speech — reused directly, never a duplicate playback path). `fftSize: 256`, `smoothingTimeConstant: 0.8`. Nine bars (`useWaveformBars(9)`), a fixed symmetric gain profile peaking at the centre index (`[0.4, 0.55, 0.72, 0.88, 1, 0.88, 0.72, 0.55, 0.4]`) so central bars react more strongly than outer ones regardless of which frequency bins happen to be loud. Each frame's raw per-bar level is exponentially interpolated toward its target (`INTERPOLATION_ALPHA = 0.35`) to avoid jitter, clamped to `[0.16, 1]` so a bar never fully disappears at rest, and applied via `el.style.transform = scaleY(...)` set directly on bar refs every animation frame — deliberately never through React state, so a live 60fps waveform never forces a re-render. When `window.AudioContext` doesn't exist, or graph construction throws, `supported` becomes `false` and `Waveform`'s `fallback` prop switches to a restrained CSS `voice-waveform-breathe` animation — still a truthful (if coarser) listening/speaking indicator, never a static bar pretending nothing is happening.

**Real microphone levels while listening**: `VoiceCaptureOverlay` builds `micSource` only while `voiceState === "listening"` and a real `micStream` was passed in, and only then does `useAudioLevels` attach — verified genuinely reactive (not just screenshotted) in a real Chromium browser via Playwright with `--use-fake-device-for-media-stream`/`--use-fake-ui-for-media-stream`: exactly one `getUserMedia` call for a whole press-hold-release gesture, an `AudioContext` genuinely created while listening, and every one of them `closed` after release (checked under React StrictMode's double-effect invocation, the harder case).

**Real TTS playback levels while speaking**: `ttsSource` only forms while `voiceState === "speaking"` and a real element is available. A `MediaElementAudioSourceNode` can only ever be created once per `<audio>` element for its lifetime (a second attempt throws, even against a fresh `AudioContext`) — `elementGraphCache` (a `WeakMap<HTMLAudioElement, {context, source}>`) remembers the one already made so every subsequent assistant reply reuses it instead of erroring, and that persistent source stays connected to `context.destination` (the thing that actually keeps the reply audible) while a fresh, disposable `AnalyserNode` fans out from the same source per utterance for analysis. Verified genuinely attached (not the CSS fallback) in a real Chromium browser via a full mock voice round trip: the waveform carried `tone-cyan` with no `is-fallback` class, no `MIC ACTIVE` indicator was present while speaking (the microphone is genuinely inactive then, preventing feedback), and the persistent TTS `AudioContext` was left intentionally `running` (reused across replies) while every per-utterance/mic `AudioContext` was `closed`.

**Reduced motion**: real bar levels keep updating under `prefers-reduced-motion` (`reducedMotion` skips the interpolation step and snaps straight to the target — a waveform communicating live input is real information, not decoration, so it doesn't stop), while purely decorative motion (the mic-active pulse, the transcribing scan sweep, the overlay's own entrance fade, the CSS breathing fallback) is zeroed by the existing single global reduced-motion rule with no new code — verified in a real browser by reading the mic-pulse dot's computed `animation-duration` under `reducedMotion: "reduce"` and confirming it was ~0.

**Three real bugs found and fixed in this pass, none of them pre-existing knowledge**:
1. `stopPushToTalk` (in both `DomainView.tsx` and `GeneralConversation.tsx`) unconditionally called `setVoiceState("idle")` immediately after `pushToTalk.stop()`. Since a genuinely-recording `pushToTalk.stop()` synchronously triggers `onRecordingComplete` → `handleRecordingComplete`, which itself calls `setVoiceState("transcribing")` in the very same synchronous batch, the later `setVoiceState("idle")` silently won (React batches same-tick `setState` calls; the last call wins) — meaning the `"transcribing"` state was never actually observable in the running product, before this pass, regardless of the waveform work. Fixed by only forcing `"idle"` when `pushToTalk.status !== "recording"` (i.e. only the genuine "permission prompt was still pending, no recorder ever started" case this reset was originally written for).
2. `audioPlaybackRef.current` (the `<audio>` element) was assigned *after* the render that showed `"speaking"`, and a ref mutation alone never triggers a re-render — so the overlay could read a stale (frequently `null`, on the first-ever utterance) element for the entire utterance. Fixed by introducing real state (`ttsAudioEl`) specifically for this prop, decoupled from the identity-preserving ref.
3. Attaching the Web Audio tap to the `<audio>` element before playback had genuinely started proved to be a real Chromium loading race — `audioEl.play()` intermittently rejected with `NotSupportedError: Failed to load because no supported source was found` purely because of when the tap was attached, not because of the audio data itself (confirmed by reproducing the same failure against a real, valid MP3 blob). Fixed by only calling `setTtsAudioEl(audioEl)` — and therefore only building the analyser graph — after `await audioEl.play()` has already resolved, never before or during.

**Current limitation, explicitly not addressed by this pass**: global voice capture is not yet available from Home or a Centre page — `usePushToTalk`/the voice state machine remain scoped to an active domain conversation or the general conversation, exactly as before this pass. See the separate bounded implementation plan for what that would require before it's built.

**Why**: a waveform that doesn't actually respond to the real microphone or the real synthesized speech would be exactly the kind of "meaningless decorative animation" CLAUDE.md §9 already prohibits — the whole point was to make the one animation most likely to run continuously during real use into a truthful one. The three bugs were each a real, user-visible defect (an invisible transcribing state, a waveform silently falling back to CSS on every first reply, an intermittent voice-round-trip failure) that a screenshot-only verification approach would never have caught — only real computed-transform reads and a real browser's actual audio-loading behavior surfaced them.
**How to apply**: `frontend/src/hooks/useAudioLevels.ts`'s doc comment is the authoritative statement of the two supported source kinds and their constraints (one `MediaElementAudioSourceNode` per element, ever) — any future audio-reactive surface (a routine's audio output, a document read-aloud feature) should reuse this hook rather than re-implementing the analyser graph. Never attach a Web Audio tap to an `<audio>` element before `.play()` has resolved — bug 3 above is the concrete reason. When adding any new voice-adjacent state transition, verify it the way this pass did: real computed styles/transforms in an actual browser, not jsdom (which has no Web Audio or CSS animation timeline at all) and not a screenshot alone.

## D79 — Global Jarvis interaction: push-to-talk from anywhere, and a deterministic three-tier command hierarchy, replacing domain-only voice and a two-tier command registry

**Context**: D78 explicitly left global voice capture unbuilt — push-to-talk existed only inside an active domain conversation or the general conversation, never from Home or a Centre page. Bernardo requested closing that gap, with one correction to the plan originally proposed for it: Centre pages must not be command-only. If a spoken transcript from a Centre page isn't a recognized command, it must still be sent to Jarvis as an ordinary question via the general conversation — never a "not recognized as a command" refusal merely because the surface has no visible conversation UI.

**Shared voice hook, one microphone at a time**: the voice state machine duplicated between `DomainView.tsx` and `GeneralConversation.tsx` (each carrying the exact same D78 fixes independently) was extracted into `frontend/src/hooks/useVoiceCapture.ts`. `App.tsx` now holds one additional instance of that same hook — the *ambient* instance — active only on Home and the six Centre pages (`ambientVoiceEligible`: true there, false whenever a domain conversation or the general conversation, each owning their own instance, is mounted, and false on a diagnostic page). Because only the currently-eligible instance's Space handler ever calls `.start()`, there is never more than one live `getUserMedia`/`MediaRecorder` session, confirmed by a regression test asserting exactly one `MediaRecorder` construction per gesture both from Home and from inside an open domain conversation.

**The ambient general conversation**: a Home/Centre voice question has no "selected conversation" to send to, so `App.tsx` lazily creates-or-reuses one real general conversation (`domain_id = NULL`, titled "Quick questions (voice)", found via `fetchGeneralConversations()` or created via `createGeneralConversation(...)`, the in-flight creation promise memoized against a duplicate race) — an ordinary row visible in `GeneralConversation.tsx`'s own list, not a hidden special case. Every ambient turn calls `sendTurn(conversationId, transcript, idempotencyKey, [])` — an explicitly empty domain array, so it never mixes in a domain automatically, matching `GeneralConversation.tsx`'s own default.

**Command safety — three tiers, all controller-owned, mapped directly to CLAUDE.md §12**: the command registry (`frontend/src/commands/registry.ts`) gained `safe_action` (sync Calendar/Health, retry connection, run an explicitly-named routine — §12 "Read" tier, executes immediately) and `confirm_required` (disconnect an integration, export data — §12 "Confirm" tier, never executed by the registry itself). Both are matched before the pre-existing `SENSITIVE_CONTROLS`/`BLOCKED_VERBS` catch-all, using the same `requires: RegExp[]`-every-match-any-order scheme; `SAFE_ACTIONS` additionally supports `excludes?: RegExp[]`, checked first, so the broad word "sync" never shadows the more specific, pre-existing "automatic sync *schedule*" toggle (a real regression test covers this exact collision, caught while writing the tests for this pass — see Errors below). `App.tsx`'s `runParsedCommand` (already the single place the Command Palette's typed commands executed) is the only place either new kind actually does anything: a `safe_action` calls the real API immediately and reports the real result via a transient, truthful status line (`commandFeedback` — an actual outcome or actual error, never decorative); a `confirm_required` action only opens `ConfirmDialog.tsx` (a new, real, portal-based `role="alertdialog"`, Escape-to-cancel), and the real API call happens only from that dialog's own Confirm button. Calendar/memory/structured-record-proposing phrases (e.g. "create a calendar event for tomorrow at 3pm") were deliberately left unmatched everywhere in the registry, so they fall through to `none` and are sent as an ordinary question — the existing Phase 8 propose→approve→execute lifecycle is what actually handles them, never bypassed by a local command shortcut.

**A real bug caught while writing tests, not in the implementation itself**: the first version of the acceptance test suite drove push-to-talk with `userEvent.keyboard("{Space>}")`. That produced a `KeyboardEvent` with `code: "Unknown"` (and `key: "Space"`, inverted from a real browser's `code: "Space"`/`key: " "`) — a quirk of this `@testing-library/user-event` version's `{Space}` DSL token, not a defect in the application (DomainView/GeneralConversation's already-shipped `event.code === "Space"` checks are correct for real physical key presses, and had simply never been exercised by keyboard in a test before, only by mouse/touch). Fixed by dispatching real `KeyboardEvent`-shaped events via `fireEvent.keyDown/keyUp(window, { code: "Space", key: " " })` in the new tests, rather than loosening the production key-matching condition to accommodate a test-harness artifact.

**Verification**: `commands/registry.test.ts` grew from 52 to 69 cases; a new `App.globalVoice.test.tsx` (9 cases) directly exercises all 13 Phase 6 global-interaction acceptance criteria Bernardo specified (core→General; Home push-to-talk→ordinary question with no domain included; Centre navigation command executes deterministically; Centre ordinary question routes to General rather than being refused; safe action executes directly; confirm-required action never executes before acceptance, and never if cancelled; a proposal-lifecycle phrase never bypasses Phase 8; exactly one `MediaRecorder` per gesture, including the domain-conversation case; Escape cancels ambient voice with nothing sent). Full suite: 166/166 frontend tests, `tsc -b`, `vite build` all pass. Backend was not touched (no new endpoints — existing sync/routine/integration/export endpoints reused as-is) and its suite could not be re-run in this container: the checked-in `backend/.venv` is a macOS-built virtualenv with a non-functional interpreter path here, and no `uv`/system `pytest` was available — a pre-existing container limitation, not a risk introduced by this change.

**Still outstanding**: real-Mac acceptance — a genuine push-to-talk voice question asked from Home (not just a domain/general conversation) and a genuine confirm-required voice command (e.g. "export data") accepted on real hardware — alongside the real-Mac visual/microphone/TTS/recovery/gateway-toolset acceptance already recorded as outstanding under D72/D78.

**Why**: model-generated text must never become authorization (CLAUDE.md §12) — every new executable path here is a fixed, controller-owned, unit-tested registry match, never a model's interpretation of a transcript, and the two genuinely consequential new action kinds either execute immediately only because they're read-only/trivially reversible, or pass through a real confirmation UI first, exactly mirroring the tier Bernardo specified for each.
**How to apply**: any future voice-reachable surface should get its own `useVoiceCapture` instance (never a second parallel state machine) and route every actionable command through `App.tsx`'s `runParsedCommand`, never handle a `safe_action`/`confirm_required` locally. A new consequential local action belongs in `CONFIRM_ACTIONS`, not a bespoke one-off dialog. When writing a test that presses-and-holds Space to drive push-to-talk, use `fireEvent.keyDown/keyUp(window, { code: "Space", key: " " })`, not `userEvent.keyboard("{Space>}")` — see the bug above.

## D80 — Operational-interface redesign: a component vocabulary for the four Centres D75 explicitly left generic, plus lighter structural work on Actions/Skills and a real caught-and-fixed contrast regression

**Context**: D75 gave every internal page a shared console *surface* (matte modules, thin violet borders, `ConsoleHeader`/`MiniCoreIndicator`) but named its own scope boundary explicitly: Memory, Integrations, Routine, and Data Management kept a stack of generically-treated `<section>`s, while only DomainView (rebuilt into the cockpit layout) and the new GeneralConversation got real per-page composition work. Bernardo's request for this pass was exactly that boundary: redesign the operational pages so they read as purpose-built instruments rather than a themed dashboard, without touching backend behavior, without repeating D75's "identical rounded card" mistake, and using purposeful structures (telemetry strips, timelines, compact rows, status clusters) suited to what each screen actually shows.

**Approach**: rather than inventing bespoke one-off layouts per screen, a small, reusable **operational-component vocabulary** was added once to `frontend/src/index.css` — `.ledger`/`.ledger-row` (compact label/value/action rows), `.timeline`/`.timeline-item` (a connected-dot history with tone-coloured dots), `.status-cluster`/`.status-cluster-item` (a provider/schedule overview strip), `.queue-item.is-pending`/`.is-approved` (left-border priority accents), `.tab-row` (a segmented-control restyle of an existing radio-button group), `.builder-surface` (a collapsed-by-default `<details>` authoring surface), `.vault-tier` (a numbered hierarchy), `.identity-grid`, and `button.action-note` — then applied to each screen wherever its actual content shape called for it. No new color, panel geometry, or motion primitive was introduced; this is composition vocabulary layered on D75's existing surface language, per `docs/DESIGN_DIRECTION.md`'s "Internal screens" doctrine (now Addendum 2 there).

**Screen-by-screen** — see `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §9k for the full technical account. In summary: Memory Centre's onboarding fields became a compact `.identity-grid`; Actions Centre split into a queue-accented "Pending review" module and a separate "History" module with structured effect/reason/source rows and a `.timeline` audit trail; Skills Centre's draft-creation form moved into a collapsed `.builder-surface` and its registry became `.ledger` rows with humanized (not raw-JSON-first) workflow steps; Integrations Centre gained compact per-provider status headers and turned every list (calendars, events, health summaries/sessions, documents) into `.ledger` rows; Routine Centre gained a `.status-cluster` schedule-overview strip and `.timeline` run history; Data Management's storage narrative became a numbered `.vault-tier` hierarchy with the secrets-excluded guarantee stated exactly once in its own banner. DomainView/GeneralConversation got one small addition beyond D75: "Save as note" is now `button.action-note` (a neutral outline), leaving "Send to Jarvis" as the only primary-styled action in that row — since only the latter actually reaches the model.

**Two accessible-name collisions caught only by running the existing tests, not by inspection**: (1) Routine Centre's new schedule-overview strip originally repeated each routine's full display name (e.g. "Morning Briefing") as its own text node, which made `screen.findByText("Morning Briefing")` in the pre-existing `RoutineCentre.test.tsx` throw on multiple matches once the per-routine section's own `<h2>` also rendered that exact text — fixed by giving the overview strip its own deliberately distinct short tag (`ROUTINE_TAGS`, e.g. "AM briefing") rather than restating the full name. (2) Integrations Centre's per-provider heading (`getByRole("heading", { name: "Google Health" })`, an exact-match assertion already in `IntegrationsCentre.test.tsx`) required keeping the `<h2>` a pure text node with the `StatusChip` rendered as a sibling, not folded into the same heading element as the original single-`<p>` composition might have tempted.

**A real, caught-before-shipping accessibility regression**: a full-sweep axe-core pass (see Verification) found two genuine WCAG AA contrast failures this pass itself introduced — `.queue-item.is-settled`'s `opacity: 0.85` de-emphasis on resolved Actions Centre rows pushed `--text-tertiary` text below 4.5:1 once composited against the page canvas, and `.vault-tier-index` (Data Management's "01"/"02" numbers) used `--text-disabled` — a token deliberately reserved for genuinely inert text — for a number that is actually informative. Both were fixed (the opacity rule removed outright, since resolved items are already distinguished by living in the separate History module and by a neutral border colour; the index switched to `--text-tertiary`), and a second axe-core sweep confirmed zero violations across all nine affected screens. A separate, purely visual defect was also caught during screenshot review before the accessibility pass even ran: Data Management's secrets-excluded guarantee was originally placed inside `ConsoleHeader`'s `meta` slot, which carries a `text-transform: uppercase` styled for short telemetry readouts — applied to a full sentence containing `<code>.env</code>`, it produced a genuinely broken-looking line wrap (the file extension split mid-word). Fixed by moving the guarantee into its own `.vault-guarantee` banner directly under the header, which also served the "stated once, not repeated" requirement.

**Verification**: full 166/166 frontend Vitest suite passes with zero test-file changes (every accessible name, placeholder, and literal test-asserted string was preserved), `tsc -b`, `vite build`, and `oxlint` (identical pre-existing warning set, no new categories) all pass. Visual/interaction verification used a temporary Playwright/Chromium harness (`/tmp/.../scratchpad/qa`, never added to `frontend/package.json`/lockfiles — same pattern as D72/D75), driving the real production build (`vite preview`) against a fixture `page.route` interception layer covering every endpoint these nine screens call (fictional data only, no real backend or accounts). Checked at 1440×900, 1280×800, 1024×768, and 820×900 for Home, a populated Domain (BODY) cockpit, General Conversation, and all six Centres: zero `document.documentElement.scrollWidth > clientWidth` overflow on any screen/viewport combination, zero browser console/page errors across the full 4-viewport sweep, a real `getComputedStyle(...).transform` read before/after a delay confirming ring rotation genuinely stops under `reducedMotion: 'reduce'`, a real keyboard-Tab focus-visible check landing a visible cyan outline on a newly-restructured Memory Centre input, and an axe-core WCAG2A/AA sweep across all nine screens (0 violations after the two fixes above, from 2 violation types beforehand). `ConfirmDialog.tsx` gained an optional `tone="danger"` prop (red border, `button.danger` Confirm button) wired from `App.tsx` only for `disconnect_integration`, its own open/close/confirm/cancel behavior otherwise unchanged and still covered by the existing `App.globalVoice.test.tsx` suite.

**Boundary respected**: no backend file was touched, no schema/migration change, no routine was enabled, no OAuth/credential/Hermes configuration was touched, Phase 11 was not started, and this pass does not mark Phase 6 complete — every real-Mac acceptance item already outstanding under D72/D77/D78/D79 (real browser, real data, real voice/TTS hardware, real Keychain/OAuth/Hermes state, one real push-to-talk question from Home, one real confirm-required voice command) remains exactly as outstanding as before.

**Why**: the specific failure mode named for this pass — Memory/Integrations/Routine/Data Management reading as generic stacked sections despite already carrying D75's surface treatment — is the same "solved every section the same way regardless of what it shows" pattern D75 itself corrected for DomainView; the fix here is the same kind of fix, just completing the set D75 left unfinished on purpose. Catching the contrast regression via axe-core (not just visual screenshot review) before shipping is exactly why `docs/DESIGN_DIRECTION.md` treats "verified via screenshots and live interaction states, not source inspection alone" as a hard requirement — a rendered composition can look correct in a screenshot while still failing a real contrast check.
**How to apply**: the operational-component vocabulary in `index.css` (`.ledger`, `.timeline`, `.status-cluster`, `.tab-row`, `.builder-surface`, `.vault-tier`) is now the first place to look for any future internal-page work that needs a compact row, a history, an overview strip, a segmented filter, a collapsed authoring surface, or a numbered hierarchy — reach for these before reintroducing ad hoc `<section>`/`<ul>` markup. Never apply `ConsoleHeader`'s `meta` slot to more than a short uppercase telemetry fragment — a full sentence or one containing inline `<code>` belongs in its own element. Never use `opacity` alone to de-emphasize text that must stay readable; prefer a border/background/placement change and re-verify with axe-core, since opacity composited over a dark canvas degrades contrast in a way that is easy to miss by eye. When a status-overview strip and a detail heading might ever show the same underlying name, give the strip its own distinct label — an axe-blind exact-text collision is easy to introduce and only tests (not visual review) reliably catch it.

## D81 — Phase 11 hardening: a container-verified security review, a broadened accessibility pass, and a genuine live restoration drill — no product behavior changed

**Context**: Bernardo asked to continue into Phase 11 (documented in `CLAUDE.md` §17 as "conduct security, portability, accessibility and recovery hardening", and in `docs/ROADMAP.md` as "harden against the full profile: loopback-only services, secret handling, schema-version safety, accessibility pass, and a repeat verified restoration drill" with acceptance "security review complete, accessibility checks pass, another full export/restore round-trip verified") — explicitly bounded to whatever is documented, safe, and testable in this Linux devcontainer, independent of the real macOS Keychain, microphone, OAuth consent screens, Google accounts, Hermes profile, and `~/JarvisData`. This session was concurrently keeping a separate, already-running fixture-backed preview (from the Phase 6 operational-redesign pass, D80) up for Bernardo's own manual visual review — every action in this entry was checked to never disrupt that shared instance.

**A genuine blocker, resolved without touching `backend/.venv`**: prior sessions (D79, D80) reported the backend test suite as un-runnable in this container because the checked-in `backend/.venv` is a macOS-built virtualenv. Rather than modifying that venv (explicitly prohibited) or accepting the limitation again, this session installed `uv` standalone (the project's own tool, per README) and used `uv python install 3.12` to provision a complete CPython — the container's bare-metal `python3` turned out to be a stripped build genuinely missing `tomllib`, `http`, and `ensurepip` (discovered mid-attempt while trying the ordinary `venv`+`pip` route, not assumed in advance). `UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT=/tmp/jarvis-backend-venv uv sync --group dev --python 3.12` then produced a fully working isolated venv outside the repository entirely. The full backend suite now runs cleanly in this container: **433/433 passed**. This is a durable capability for future container sessions, not a one-off workaround — see `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §12 for the exact commands.

**Security review** — an audit against the current code, not a rewrite, covering exactly the three areas ROADMAP.md names:
1. *Loopback-only services*: every `uvicorn`/script invocation binds `127.0.0.1` explicitly (grepped the whole backend and `scripts/` for `0.0.0.0` — none found); `CORSMiddleware` allows exactly one non-wildcard configured origin with `allow_credentials=False`.
2. *Secret handling*: grepped both `backend/app` and `frontend/src` for hardcoded secret literals, secret-bearing log statements, and any `localStorage`/`sessionStorage` reference. None found — notably, the entire frontend source contains zero `console.log`/`console.debug`/`console.info` calls and zero browser-storage reads or writes of any kind, so CLAUDE.md §5's "never store authoritative personal data in browser storage" holds structurally rather than merely by convention. Confirmed `HERMES_API_BEARER_TOKEN` is read only inside `HermesProvider`'s own header construction and appears in no router's response path.
3. *Schema-version safety*: read `app/migration_info.py` directly rather than trusting the existing architecture-doc description — `is_known_revision()` genuinely enumerates every revision via `ScriptDirectory.walk_revisions()` (not a "matches head" shortcut), so a real older revision restores/upgrades cleanly while a genuinely unrecognized one is rejected with an explicit reason; `test_rejects_unsupported_future_schema_revision` already covers this exact path.

**One adjacent finding, reported rather than fixed**: `create_app()`'s CORS `allow_methods=["GET", "POST"]` omits `PUT`, confirmed live via a direct `TestClient` OPTIONS request (`access-control-allow-methods: GET, POST`, and the actual preflight check returns 400 for a requested `PUT`). This blocks a cross-origin (`npm run dev`, port 5173→8000) call to any `PUT` endpoint — domain-summary edit, integration schedule, routine schedule — purely in the dev workflow; Phase 7's production static-file serving is same-origin and never triggers a CORS preflight, which is exactly why this was never previously observed or reported. This is a correctness gap in the *more-restrictive* direction, not a vulnerability, and fixing it was judged outside this phase's explicitly bounded scope ("do not expand Phase 11 beyond its documented scope") — recorded here for Bernardo to decide on, not silently left undocumented.

**Accessibility pass**: the axe-core (WCAG 2 A/AA) sweep already run once during D80 for the six redesigned Centres was broadened to Home, a populated Domain view, an *empty* Domain view, the general conversation, the command palette, the 404 diagnostic, and the Controller Offline diagnostic (the last two via a throwaway Playwright browser context — the 404 by direct navigation, Controller Offline via client-side `page.route` interception, so the real shared preview instance Bernardo was actively reviewing was never touched, restarted, or disrupted). **Zero violations across every screen checked.** A keyboard-only Tab sweep from Home's initial focus state confirmed a complete, logical tab order (command palette → Systems → the Jarvis core → the six domain nodes in their fixed order) with visible focus throughout — not merely "no axe violation for focus-visible" but an actual traversal read back and inspected.

**Restoration drill**: a genuine live drill, not a re-run of the existing pytest round-trip tests — two fresh isolated `JARVIS_DATA_DIR`s, two real `uvicorn` subprocess instances (never `TestClient`). Installation A was populated via real HTTP calls (no Hermes/model contact, matching the container boundary) with: a domain conversation and note, a general conversation and note, a global memory, a domain memory edited to a second version, a domain summary, a structured record, one action proposal taken fully through propose→approve→execute, one skill created→activated→invoked→approved→executed (exercising the complete Phase 8 skill-invocation-creates-a-proposal path end to end), one action proposal deliberately left `proposed` and never approved, and one routine schedule explicitly enabled. `jarvis-cli export` → `jarvis-cli validate` → `jarvis-cli restore` into installation B, then 15 explicit assertions against B's live API — **all 15 passed**, including both restore-time safety-forcing behaviors this codebase specifically implements: the deliberately-pending proposal was genuinely forced to `expired` (never left `proposed`/`approved`/`executing`), and the enabled routine schedule was genuinely forced back to disabled. A post-restore write succeeded, and a full stop/restart of installation B's backend process preserved everything written both before and after the restore.

**What this establishes and what remains**: none of the security/accessibility/restoration properties claimed throughout Phases 1-10B were taken on faith — each was independently re-verified against the current code and schema (through migration `0011`) in a clean environment with no memory of how the original claims were produced, and all held. Nothing here is new product surface. Still outstanding, genuinely requiring real hardware/software this container cannot provide: `KeychainCredentialStore` has never been exercised against a real macOS Keychain (only `FakeCredentialStore`, the same pattern every prior phase's tests use); no manual screen-reader (VoiceOver) pass was performed; the Hermes gateway's own loopback-only binding is external configuration, already live-verified in an earlier phase and not re-checked here since no real gateway runs in this container.

**Verification**: full backend suite 433/433 (isolated Linux venv, real `uv sync` — no macOS `.venv` touched), full frontend suite 166/166, `tsc -b`, `vite build`, `oxlint` all pass (no code changed by this pass, so these are confirmations, not new results). No schema/migration change, no routine enabled on Bernardo's real installation, no OAuth/credential/Hermes configuration touched, no Git operation performed. Phase 6 remains not marked complete — untouched by this pass.

**Why**: a hardening phase's value comes specifically from re-verifying claims independently rather than re-asserting them from memory — CLAUDE.md's own evaluation doctrine ("verified via screenshots and live interaction states, not source inspection alone") applies equally to security and restoration claims, not just visual ones. Solving the `backend/.venv` blocker properly (a real isolated `uv`-managed environment) rather than reporting the same limitation a third time is the same principle applied to tooling.
**How to apply**: any future container session needing the real backend test suite should reach for `UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT=<tmp path> uv sync --group dev --python 3.12` (see `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §12) rather than assuming it's impossible or attempting to modify `backend/.venv`. The CORS `allow_methods` gap is a known, deliberately-unfixed finding — resolve it only on explicit request, by adding `PUT` (and `DELETE` if a future endpoint needs it) to `create_app()`'s `allow_methods` list, and add a regression test asserting the preflight succeeds for the specific origin/method pairs the dev frontend actually uses.

## D82 — Fixed the CORS PUT gap D81 found: an isolated, non-wildcard correction

**Context**: D81's security review found and deliberately left unfixed (as out of that phase's own bounded scope) a CORS correctness gap: `create_app()`'s `allow_methods=["GET", "POST"]` omitted `PUT`, so a cross-origin `npm run dev` (port 5173) call to any real `PUT` endpoint — `PUT /api/domains/{slug}/summary`, `PUT /api/integrations/{provider}/schedule`, `PUT /api/routines/{routine_type}/schedule` — failed its browser preflight. Bernardo asked for this fixed now, as one small, isolated correction, with explicit requirements: identify the exact cause, add `PUT` only (no wildcard origin or method), preserve every other existing policy property, add focused regression tests, run only the directly-affected backend tests, and record the correction here without touching product features, integrations, credentials, routines, migrations, Hermes configuration, frontend design, or Git — and without closing Phase 6 or Phase 11.

**Exact cause**: Starlette's `CORSMiddleware` treats a cross-origin non-simple request (any `PUT`, always) as requiring a preflight `OPTIONS` request first; the browser sends `Access-Control-Request-Method: PUT`, and the middleware only echoes back the methods present in its own `allow_methods` list. With `["GET", "POST"]`, `PUT` was never in that list, so the preflight response omitted it and the browser correctly refused to send the real `PUT` request — confirmed directly via a `TestClient` OPTIONS call before this fix (400, `access-control-allow-methods: GET, POST`) and after (200, `access-control-allow-methods: GET, POST, PUT`).

**The fix** (`backend/app/main.py::create_app`): changed exactly one line, `allow_methods=["GET", "POST"]` → `allow_methods=["GET", "POST", "PUT"]`, with a short inline comment pointing at this entry. Nothing else in the `CORSMiddleware` call changed: `allow_origins=[settings.cors_origin]` remains the single configured origin (never a list with more than one entry, never `"*"`), `allow_credentials=False` is unchanged, `allow_headers=["Content-Type"]` is unchanged. No route, dependency, schema, migration, credential path, routine, or Hermes-related code was touched.

**Regression tests** (`backend/tests/test_cors_policy.py`, new file, 4 cases, using the existing `client` fixture from `tests/conftest.py`):
1. `test_preflight_for_put_succeeds_from_the_approved_dev_origin` — an `OPTIONS` preflight for `PUT /api/domains/body/summary` from `http://localhost:5173` (the configured `cors_origin` default) now returns 200 with that exact origin echoed back and `PUT` present in `access-control-allow-methods`.
2. `test_existing_allowed_methods_still_work` — the same preflight check for `GET` and `POST` against `/api/domains` still succeeds, proving the fix didn't disturb the pre-existing methods.
3. `test_put_is_not_available_to_an_unapproved_origin` — the identical `PUT` preflight from `http://evil.example.com` still returns 400 with no `access-control-allow-origin` header at all, proving the fix widened *which method*, never *which origin*.
4. `test_cors_policy_has_no_wildcard_origin_or_method` — asserts the resolved preflight response's origin is exactly the configured one (never `"*"`), the resolved method set is exactly `{"GET", "POST", "PUT"}` (never `"*"`), and `access-control-allow-credentials` is never `"true"` — a guard against a future edit accidentally widening this policy rather than extending it.

**Verification**: `tests/test_cors_policy.py` (4/4) passes in isolation; the full backend suite was also re-run as a safety net (not required to isolate the change, but cheap and available now that D81 set up a working isolated Linux `uv` venv) — **437/437 passed** (433 pre-existing + these 4). Frontend tests were deliberately not re-run, per Bernardo's explicit instruction — no frontend file was touched by this correction, so there is nothing there to regress.

**Boundary respected**: no product feature, integration, credential, routine, migration, Hermes configuration, or frontend file was touched; no Git operation was performed; Phase 6 and Phase 11 both remain open exactly as before, pending only their previously-documented real-Mac acceptance items — this correction closes a documentation-tracked finding, not either phase itself.

**Why**: D81 correctly judged that fixing a discovered gap wasn't automatically in-scope for a hardening phase whose acceptance criteria were already fixed and being verified — reporting it and letting Bernardo decide when to act on it was the right call at the time, exactly as CLAUDE.md's phase-discipline rule intends. Fixing it now, on explicit request, as its own isolated change (not folded back into D81's already-closed narrative) keeps the append-only decision log honest about when each thing actually happened.
**How to apply**: this is the reference example for "a security-review finding gets fixed later, on request" — record the fix as a new entry that references the one that found it, never edit the original finding's text. Any future CORS change should keep `allow_methods`/`allow_origins` as explicit lists (never `"*"`, especially never `"*"` combined with `allow_credentials=True`) and extend `tests/test_cors_policy.py` rather than starting a new ad hoc CORS test elsewhere.

## D83 — V1 reliability and intelligence audit, backend: an adversarial senior-engineering review across failure/recovery, memory/context, and scale — three genuine defects found and fixed, extensive existing coverage confirmed still correct

**Context**: Bernardo requested a V1 reliability and intelligence audit treating the implemented system as the subject of an adversarial review, across four workstreams (failure/recovery, natural-language command safety, memory/context, scale), with an explicit instruction to inspect existing tests first (62 backend test files, ~437 test functions, already found to be extensive), then implement missing tests and fix only genuine defects revealed by them — no speculative features, no frontend redesign. This entry covers the backend half (failure/recovery, memory/context, scale); D84 covers the frontend half (natural-language command safety).

### Risk register (backend), ranked by severity × likelihood

| # | Finding | Severity | Likelihood | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | An action proposal crashed/killed mid-`execute_action` (between the "executing" commit and the final succeeded/failed commit) was permanently stuck — no code path anywhere resolved it on an ordinary restart, only `import_service._expire_stale_action_proposals` during a restore. | Medium (a real mutation's true outcome becomes permanently unresolvable via the UI; for a Calendar write, the external effect's success/failure is genuinely unknown) | Low-Medium (requires a crash/kill/sleep during a narrow window; a laptop is more likely to sleep mid-network-call than a server) | **Fixed** |
| 2 | No explicit `PRAGMA busy_timeout` — SQLite's documented default is 0ms, meaning a second writer (the FastAPI request path vs. the background `SchedulerRuntime`, which always run as independent connections against the same file) could raise `database is locked` as an unhandled 500 on any platform whose linked SQLite build doesn't patch this default away. | Medium (an ordinary concurrent-use unhandled 500) | Low-Medium (platform-dependent; this container's own SQLite build happened to already default to 5000ms, a real and important nuance recorded below) | **Fixed** |
| 3 | Google Calendar's `_normalize_event` (direct `item["id"]` indexing) and several Google Health metric `extract()` functions (`v.get("distance", {}).get(...)`, which only substitutes the `{}` default when the key is *absent*, not when explicitly `null`) could raise an unhandled `KeyError`/`AttributeError` on a malformed or partial API response, aborting the *entire* sync — contradicting Google Health's own documented "one metric's failure never aborts the rest" guarantee — and escaping the narrow `except (IntegrationError, GoogleCalendarError/GoogleHealthError)` clauses as an unhandled 500. | Medium (breaks a guarantee the product's own documentation makes; a real external API returning an explicit `null` for an absent metric is a realistic, not exotic, shape) | Low-Medium | **Fixed** |
| 4 | "No relevant context" behavior (a brand-new domain with zero memories/records/summary) was not explicitly covered by a named test, though every retrieval section in `context_builder.py` is already conditional. | Low (design already correct) | N/A | **Confirmed safe — test added, no code change needed** |
| 5 | Restoration safety-forcing (`_expire_stale_action_proposals`, `_disconnect_all_integrations`, `_disable_all_integration_schedules`, `_disable_all_routine_schedules`), schema-version rejection (`is_known_revision`), secret handling, and loopback-only binding — all independently re-verified against the current code during this pass. | N/A | N/A | **Confirmed safe, no defect** |
| 6 | Scale: a fictional full year of heavy personal use (240 conversations, 1,920 messages, 745 memories, 360 structured records, 150 action proposals, 600 routine runs, 15 documents) produces a 1.98MB database; FTS search, context construction, and conversation loading all complete in single-digit milliseconds; export/validate/restore complete in under 100ms combined. | N/A | N/A | **Confirmed safe — enormous headroom versus this app's actual single-user target; no scale defect found** |
| 7 | Interrupted export/backup (process killed mid-write) — already correctly mitigated by design (temp-file + `os.replace()` atomic rename, `flock` for export's own concurrent-run guard, released automatically on process death) and covered by existing tests (`export_fails_safely_on_inconsistent_database`, `existing_installation_preserved_after_simulated_failed_restore`). One minor, deliberately deferred item: a `.tmp-*` file left behind by a genuinely interrupted export/backup is never automatically cleaned up (pure disk hygiene, never picked up as a valid export since it doesn't match the real naming pattern, never a correctness or security issue). | Low | Low | **Deliberately deferred — not a defect, disk hygiene only** |

### Fix #1 — interrupted "executing" action proposals recovered at startup

`action_service.expire_interrupted_executions(session)` (new function): sweeps every proposal still in `executing` and marks it `failed` with an explicit, honest error summary ("the real-world outcome is unknown... verify the target system directly") — never a fabricated `succeeded`. Wired into `app/main.py`'s lifespan startup (after domain/skill seeding, before the backup due-check), wrapped in try/except so a failure here can never block startup, matching the existing due-backup-check pattern. Verified two ways: a direct unit test (`test_action_service.py::test_interrupted_execution_is_recovered_at_startup_not_left_stuck_forever`) simulating the exact crash state and calling the function directly, and a full end-to-end integration test (`test_actions_api.py::test_a_proposal_stuck_executing_by_a_crash_is_recovered_on_the_next_real_startup`) using `restart_client_factory` to prove `app.main`'s real lifespan — not just the unit-level function — actually performs the recovery on a genuine second app startup.

### Fix #2 — explicit SQLite `busy_timeout`, with an honest correction along the way

`app/database.py`'s existing `PRAGMA foreign_keys=ON` connect-event listener now also sets `PRAGMA busy_timeout=5000`. **An important finding while writing the regression tests**: this container's particular linked SQLite build already defaults to a 5000ms busy timeout with *no* pragma set at all — some distributions patch this away from upstream SQLite's documented 0ms default — which meant a first draft of the test (checking only "does a brief overlap succeed without raising") passed identically whether the fix's `PRAGMA` line was present or commented out, i.e. it was **not actually proving the fix worked**, only that this specific platform's ambient default happened to be generous. Caught by disabling the fix and re-running the tests, exactly the discipline this audit was commissioned to apply to the rest of the codebase. The tests were rewritten (`test_database_concurrency.py`) to be conclusive regardless of platform: one test explicitly forces `busy_timeout=0` on both connections (proving the underlying failure mode is real and this test file's locking mechanism can detect it, independent of any platform default), a second explicitly forces a non-zero value (proving the fix's mechanism works), and a third asserts the app's own connect-event listener produces the exact documented value (proving the app's own code path is what's responsible, not coincidence). All three re-verified by disabling the real fix in `app/database.py` and confirming the appropriate test then fails, then restoring it and confirming green again.

### Fix #3 — malformed/partial Google Calendar and Google Health responses

Per-data-point isolation added to all three `fetch_health_data` loops (`ROLLUP_METRICS`, `DAILY_LIST_METRICS`, `SESSION_METRICS` in `app/providers/google_health.py`): a `(KeyError, IndexError, TypeError, ValueError, AttributeError)` catch around each point's `extract()`/`parser()` call records that single point's failure into `partial_failures[metric.key]` and continues — preserving every other metric's already-successfully-parsed data in the same sync, matching the integration's own documented guarantee. A matching whole-sync backstop was added to both `sync_google_calendar` and `sync_google_health` in `app/integration_service.py` (the same exception tuple added to their existing `except (IntegrationError, GoogleCalendarError/GoogleHealthError)` clauses), so any *other*, not-yet-isolated parsing gap anywhere in either sync (e.g. `_normalize_event`'s direct `item["id"]` indexing) is still converted to a clean, recorded `IntegrationError` rather than escaping as an unhandled 500. New tests: `test_integration_service.py::test_sync_google_health_malformed_data_point_does_not_abort_other_metrics` (an explicit JSON `null` for `"distance"` alongside a normal `"steps"` response — asserts `steps` still populates and the sync is truthfully `"partial"`, not `"ok"` or a crash) and `::test_sync_google_calendar_malformed_event_recorded_as_error_not_unhandled_exception` (an event object missing `"id"` — asserts a clean `IntegrationError`, not an unhandled exception). Both re-verified by reverting the fix and confirming the malformed-health test fails as expected (the calendar fix wasn't separately reverted, since its mechanism — widening an already-tested except clause — is a simpler, lower-risk change than the per-point isolation).

### Coverage added without a code change

`test_context_builder.py::test_a_brand_new_domain_with_nothing_in_it_produces_a_valid_truthful_context` — a domain with zero memories/records/summary, sending its very first message, was traced through `build_context` and confirmed already safe by construction (every retrieval section is conditional on non-empty results); this test closes the audit brief's named "no-relevant-context behavior" item as explicitly verified rather than merely believed correct by inspection.

### Scale testing (workstream 4)

`tests/test_scale_benchmarks.py` (new): generates a fictional dataset representing a genuinely heavy year of personal use — deliberately generous versus Bernardo's actual expected usage, never real data — then benchmarks every operation the audit brief names. Measured on this container (a personal machine should perform at least this well):

| Operation | Result |
|---|---|
| Dataset generation (2,500+ rows across every subsystem) | 2.08s |
| Database size | 1.98 MB |
| Cold engine + first query ("startup") | 1.2 ms |
| FTS memory search at full volume (745 memories) | 0.4 ms |
| Context construction (a year of BODY history) | 6.8 ms |
| Conversation list (40 conversations) | 0.4 ms |
| Message load (8 messages) | 1.1 ms |
| Export | 50 ms |
| Archive size (compressed) | 0.42 MB |
| Validate | 20 ms |
| Isolated restore | 30 ms |

No scale-related defect found — every measurement is one to three orders of magnitude inside this test file's own (already generous) upper bounds. This is itself the audit finding for workstream 4: Jarvis's SQLite-based, single-user architecture has enormous headroom at realistic personal-scale volumes.

### A durable capability this audit produced as a side effect

Prior sessions (D79, D80) reported the backend test suite as un-runnable in a Linux devcontainer because the checked-in `backend/.venv` is a macOS-built virtualenv. This session resolved that properly rather than reporting the same limitation again: `uv` installed standalone, `uv python install 3.12` provisioned a complete CPython (the container's bare-metal `python3` is a stripped build genuinely missing `tomllib`/`http`/`ensurepip`), and `UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT=/tmp/jarvis-backend-venv uv sync --group dev --python 3.12` produced a fully working isolated venv, never touching `backend/.venv`. The full backend suite (451/451 after this audit's additions, up from 433 before D81) now runs cleanly in this container — see `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §12 for the exact commands, now used across D81, D82, and this entry.

### Verification

Full backend suite: **451/451 pass** (437 after D82, +14 from this audit: 1 interrupted-execution unit test, 1 interrupted-execution HTTP integration test, 3 database-concurrency tests, 2 malformed-response tests, 1 no-relevant-context test, 6 scale benchmarks). No frontend file touched by this half of the audit.

### Remaining Mac-only checks

Unchanged from D81: `KeychainCredentialStore` has never been exercised against a real macOS Keychain (only `FakeCredentialStore`); no manual screen-reader pass; the Hermes gateway's own loopback-only binding is external configuration already live-verified in an earlier phase. This audit adds no new Mac-only item — every fix and every piece of new coverage here runs entirely against fakes/isolated SQLite, as required.

### Deliberately deferred

The `.tmp-*` orphaned-file disk-hygiene item (row 7 above) — genuinely never a correctness or security issue, and adding automatic cleanup would be new functionality, not a bug fix, so it was left unfixed pending an explicit request. No other backend risk was deliberately deferred; everything else surfaced by this audit was either fixed or confirmed already safe.

**Boundary respected**: no new broad dependency was introduced; no forward-only migration was needed (no genuine data-model defect required one — the three fixes are all pure logic/configuration changes); no real credential, integration, schedule, or personal data was touched; no Git operation was performed; Phase 6 and Phase 11 remain exactly as open as before.

**Why**: an "adversarial senior-engineering review" only has value if it's willing to find its own review flawed — the busy_timeout test's initial vacuous pass, caught only by deliberately disabling the fix and re-running, is the clearest example in this entry of why. The same discipline (revert-and-confirm-red before trusting a green test) was applied to every fix in this entry, not assumed from the test's intent alone.
**How to apply**: any new external-API integration should assume a field can be present-but-`null`, not only absent — `.get(key, default) or default` (not `.get(key, default)` alone) is the safe idiom when a value could legitimately be `null`, as already used correctly in `app/providers/hermes.py`'s `data.get("usage") or {}`. Any new controller-owned mutation with a multi-step execution (like `execute_action`) should consider what a crash mid-execution leaves behind, and whether an ordinary restart — not just a restore — needs to recover it. Before trusting a new concurrency/locking regression test, disable the fix it's meant to guard and confirm the test actually goes red first.

## D84 — V1 reliability and intelligence audit, frontend: two genuine natural-language command-safety defects found and fixed (negation and questions resolving identically to their affirmative command)
