## D104 — V1 Native macOS Packaging, Stage 1: Jarvis.app installed and verified on the real Mac

**Context**: with V1 frozen at Phase 12F (D103), Bernardo asked for the existing dev-mode React/Vite + FastAPI application to be packaged as a real native macOS `.app` he can launch from Finder/Dock without Chrome, a terminal, or a manually-entered URL — explicitly scoped as "Stage 1: Installed App and Lifecycle," not a new product phase and not distribution. He later explicitly ruled out notarization, a paid Apple Developer account, a public-distribution DMG, an updater, and App Store distribution for this project entirely (private, single-Mac use only) — there is no "Stage 2" unless he asks for one later.

**Architecture chosen**: Tauri 2 (`frontend/src-tauri/`) provides the native window, macOS menu-bar tray, single-instance enforcement, and (disabled-by-default) Launch-at-Login. The existing FastAPI backend is packaged as a self-contained PyInstaller onefile arm64 sidecar (`backend/packaging/entrypoint.py` + `backend/packaging/jarvis_backend.spec`) — no second backend, no duplicate database, no parallel launcher. The native window loads the existing production frontend build through the sidecar's own local origin (`http://127.0.0.1:8000/`) exactly as `scripts/jarvisctl.sh` already does — same-origin, so no CORS/URL-entry changes were needed anywhere in the existing frontend code. A small `offline.html` (bundled as Tauri's own `frontendDist`) is the window's initial content, replaced by a Rust-driven `window.navigate()` to the real origin once the sidecar reports healthy — giving a truthful native "Jarvis Controller" starting/error diagnostic (CLAUDE.md's "no fake activity" rule applied to the native shell itself) for the brief window before the backend exists to serve its own diagnostic. `jarvisctl.sh` is untouched and still works as the documented dev/recovery fallback.

**Process ownership and lifecycle** (`frontend/src-tauri/src/sidecar.rs`, `lib.rs`): on startup, a background thread probes `GET /api/health` on 127.0.0.1:8000 — a response that isn't genuinely Jarvis's own JSON body is treated as a real port conflict (reported truthfully, never silently adopted); a healthy response means an already-running backend (dev-mode `jarvisctl.sh`, or a prior launch of this same app) is reused and this process never touches its lifecycle; only when nothing answers does this app spawn and *own* the sidecar (`SidecarState.owned_child`, `Mutex<Option<CommandChild>>` — `None` for a reused backend, so Quit can never kill a process this app didn't start). `tauri-plugin-single-instance` (registered first, per Tauri's own requirement) makes a second launch focus the existing window instead of spawning a second instance. Closing the window hides it (`WindowEvent::CloseRequested` → `prevent_close()` + `hide()`) rather than quitting, so scheduled integration syncs and routines keep running with the window hidden; a macOS "Reopen" event (Dock-icon click on a running, windowless app) restores it. A tray menu offers Open/Hide/System Status/Sync Integrations/Export Portable Jarvis Backup/Restore from Jarvis Export…/Reveal Jarvis Data Folder/Launch at Login (unchecked by default)/Quit. Quit sends **SIGTERM** to the tracked sidecar pid (not `CommandChild::kill()`'s bare SIGKILL — see the defect below), waits up to 2s, escalates to SIGKILL only if needed, then independently re-checks whoever is still bound to port 8000 and kills it only if its own executable path is an exact match for the bundled sidecar binary (mirroring `jarvisctl.sh`'s own "verified PID" pattern) — this path is covered by `tauri::RunEvent::ExitRequested`/`Exit` as well as the tray's own Quit item, so Cmd+Q and Dock▸Quit clean up identically.

**Two genuine defects found and fixed during real-device verification** (not merely in review):
1. **Undrained stdio deadlock**: `tauri-plugin-shell`'s `Command::spawn()` always pipes the child's stdout/stderr; the returned event receiver was originally dropped immediately, and once Alembic's verbose first-run migration log exceeded the OS pipe buffer, the child blocked on `write()` forever with no visible error. Fixed by permanently draining the receiver on a spawned async task for the sidecar's whole lifetime.
2. **Orphaned sidecar on Quit**: `CommandChild::kill()` sends `SIGKILL` straight to the tracked pid, which for a PyInstaller *onefile* sidecar is the bootloader stub, not the real unpacked Python/uvicorn process it supervises — SIGKILL gives the bootloader no chance to relay anything, so the real process was found reparented to `launchd` and still bound to port 8000 after Quit, silently breaking every later launch's reuse-vs-spawn probe. Fixed with the SIGTERM-first + verified-port-owner approach described above (PyInstaller's bootloader does forward SIGTERM to its child — this is documented upstream behavior).

Both were caught by actually launching the packaged `.app` and inspecting real process state (`ps`, `lsof`), not by code review alone — real-device verification is why they surfaced at all. A frozen-path regression was also caught before it ever shipped: `app/main.py`'s frontend-dist resolution originally assumed `Path(__file__)` still pointed at the real source tree, which is meaningless inside a frozen bundle; fixed to resolve from `sys._MEIPASS` under PyInstaller onefile, with two focused regression tests (`backend/tests/test_static_frontend.py`) covering both the MEIPASS and no-MEIPASS-fallback cases.

**Code signing — a genuinely new problem this session had to solve live**: ad-hoc signing (macOS's default with no configured identity) gives every PyInstaller rebuild a different CDHash, so each rebuild made macOS treat the sidecar as a brand-new, never-before-seen binary asking to read the real `jarvis.google_calendar`/`jarvis.google_health` Keychain items — a fresh authorization prompt every single time, which Bernardo experienced directly and found genuinely disruptive before the fix landed. **Root cause was two-layered, not one**: (1) no stable signing identity, and, once that was fixed, (2) Tauri's own signing step applies **hardened runtime** whenever a real (non-adhoc) identity is configured, and hardened runtime's default Library Validation then rejected loading the bundled `Python.framework` and every native extension module (numpy, ctranslate2, onnxruntime, …) because none of them carry Bernardo's own signing identity — confirmed directly via a captured error (`code signature ... not valid for use in process: mapping process and mapped file ... have different Team IDs`) and a stack sample showing the process genuinely blocked inside `SecItemCopyMatching`, not looping.

Fix, proposed to Bernardo in full before creating anything and only executed after his explicit "Yes, create it": a **local self-signed code-signing certificate** ("Jarvis Local Dev", 10-year validity, `codeSigning` EKU only) generated with `openssl` and imported into the login keychain via `security import ... -T /usr/bin/codesign`, then trusted for the `codeSign` policy specifically (`security add-trusted-cert -p codeSign`, scoped to the login keychain, not System) — no Apple Developer account, no network call, purely local to this one Mac. `frontend/src-tauri/tauri.conf.json`'s `bundle.macOS.signingIdentity` now pins this identity so every rebuild carries the *same* designated requirement (keyed off the certificate, not the binary's content hash), and `frontend/src-tauri/entitlements.plist` sets `com.apple.security.cs.disable-library-validation` and `com.apple.security.cs.allow-unsigned-executable-memory` — an explicitly documented interim choice that is safe specifically because nothing here is notarized or distributed elsewhere; it would be the wrong choice for a real Developer-ID/notarized build, which this project does not intend to produce. Verified empirically, twice in a row, with zero prompts on both a Terminal-exec relaunch and a full `open`-triggered GUI relaunch after the identity was approved once; a further approval was still needed the first time the installed copy was launched from its new `/Applications` location (a genuinely new install location, not a re-signing regression) and was likewise one-time.

**Installed-app acceptance, performed against the real Mac and real `~/JarvisData`** (never a synthetic install): `/Applications/Jarvis.app` (90MB, arm64, signed with the stable local identity) opens with no Chrome, Safari, or manually-entered URL at any point; exactly one `app` process and its own sidecar exist at a time; the backend reaches healthy in 8–16s; Google Calendar and Google Health stayed `connected`/`ok` throughout every relaunch — no OAuth consent screen was ever triggered; window close→Dock-icon reopen works; Quit stops the app-owned sidecar with port 8000 confirmed free and zero orphaned processes afterward (re-verified after the fix above); Hermes stayed loopback-only with zero enabled toolsets throughout (unchanged by any step in this session); `scripts/jarvisctl.sh` continues to work unmodified as the dev/recovery fallback.

**Explicitly deferred, staying V1-scope-frozen, not Stage 2**: Launch-at-Login was left disabled (Bernardo may enable it himself later — not exercised as a real login-time test in this session); installed-app VoiceOver and the 1280×800/1024×768/820×900 viewport inspection remain exactly as outstanding as D103 left them, now against the shape that actually ships.

**Boundary respected**: no OAuth flow was initiated or altered; the only Keychain change was the one new local self-signed certificate, proposed explicitly and created only after Bernardo's explicit "yes"; no real Google Calendar/Health credential was read, displayed, or altered — only Keychain's own one-time UI-driven authorization prompts were involved, each approved by Bernardo directly; no Hermes configuration was changed; no integration/routine schedule was changed; no notarization, DMG, updater, or distribution work was attempted; no Git operation.

## D105 — Cross-Mac portability: guarded in-app Restore, `GET /api/data-dir`, and an installed-app migration acceptance drill

**Context**: immediately following D104, Bernardo added a non-negotiable requirement that Jarvis.app remain portable between his Macs — Jarvis.app contains only application code, all persistent context stays in `~/JarvisData` outside the bundle, and rebuilding/replacing/moving Jarvis.app must never overwrite, relocate, or silently initialize over existing user data. This was already true of the underlying architecture (nothing in D104's packaging work changes how `app/config.py` resolves `JARVIS_DATA_DIR`, and the sidecar reuses the exact same real directory the dev-mode backend already used throughout this session's own testing) — what was missing was a *convenient* way to actually move data between two Macs without a terminal.

**Reused, not reinvented**: the portable archive format, its manifest/checksum/schema validation, and every restore-time safety behavior (forced-disconnected integrations, forced-disabled integration/routine schedules, interrupted focus sessions, rollback-on-failure, secrets-never-included) are exactly Phase 2's existing `app/export_service.py`/`app/import_service.py` — the same `restore_archive()` function the CLI (`jarvis-cli restore`) already called. No second archive format or parallel restore path was created.

**What's new**: `POST /api/restore` (`app/routers/data_management.py`) exposes that same function over HTTP for the first time, gated by an explicit `confirm` field mirroring the CLI's own `--confirm` exactly — refuses with `409` if the target already has data and `confirm` wasn't set, `422` on an invalid archive. Restoring over a *live* running instance's own database (the actual scenario the native UI performs) required one real fix: the shared SQLAlchemy `Engine`'s connection pool is explicitly `.dispose()`d immediately before the database file underneath it is replaced, so every later checkout — this process never rebuilds the `Engine`/`sessionmaker` objects themselves, only clears their pooled connections — transparently opens the *restored* file instead of a stale handle to the file that used to be there; verified by a real regression test that restores over the test client's own live database mid-suite and then confirms the same process keeps serving requests correctly afterward (`tests/test_data_management_api.py::test_restore_endpoint_restores_over_the_live_database`). A small new `GET /api/data-dir` returns the resolved absolute `JARVIS_DATA_DIR` path (non-secret — already shown as UI copy) for both the Data Management screen's own display and the native tray's "Reveal Jarvis Data Folder" action.

**Native UI wiring**: Data Management's former CLI-only "Restore on another machine" module (a deliberate Phase 2 safety choice, explicitly superseded here by Bernardo's own direct request) is replaced with a real guarded flow — choose a file, it's validated immediately, an explicit warning plus a required "I understand this replaces this Mac's current Jarvis data" checkbox gates a "Restore now" button, and the result reports exactly what was restored and that integrations/schedules are now disabled pending review. The "Where your data lives" module now shows the real resolved path (fetched live, not hardcoded) and an explicit Mac-migration procedure. The macOS tray gained three items: **Export Portable Jarvis Backup** (a direct one-click `POST /api/export` — safe and idempotent, no confirmation needed) and **Reveal Jarvis Data Folder** (fetches `/api/data-dir`, then `open`s it in Finder) act immediately; **Restore from Jarvis Export…** deliberately only brings Jarvis to the front rather than performing the destructive action headlessly from a menu click — the guarded confirmation flow lives entirely in Data Management, matching the same reasoning already applied to "System Status" in D104.

**Installed-app migration acceptance drill, performed through the actual installed `/Applications/Jarvis.app` and its real production `/api/export` path, restored into a freshly-created isolated `/tmp` directory, never Bernardo's real `~/JarvisData`**: the export's manifest (`schema_revision: 0018`) and checksum both validated cleanly via `jarvis-cli validate`; `jarvis-cli restore --target <isolated tmp dir>` (no `--confirm`, since the isolated target was empty) restored it; representative row counts confirmed data from every current feature survived intact (domains 6, conversations 3, structured_records 1, calendar_event_cache 1, google_health_daily_summaries 37, action_proposals 2, briefing_snapshots 201, focus_sessions 9, recall_fts 25, research_workspaces 1, research_evidence 2, decisions 2, decision_final_versions 2, routine_schedules 3, skills 4); both integrations confirmed `disconnected` with `scopes_json` cleared to `[]`; all three routine schedules and both integration sync schedules confirmed `enabled=0`; a `strings` scan of the restored database and the archive's own file listing both confirmed no token/secret pattern and no leaked absolute host path; a real `CREATE TABLE`/`INSERT`/`SELECT`/`DROP` round-trip confirmed the restored database genuinely writable, not just present. The real `~/JarvisData` was reconfirmed unchanged throughout (schema still `0018`, `conversations` count unchanged, `/api/health` still `200`) and the isolated target was deleted afterward.

**Testing**: 6 new focused backend tests (`test_data_dir_endpoint_returns_resolved_isolated_path`, four `test_restore_endpoint_*` cases covering confirm-gating/live-restore/forced-disconnect/invalid-archive, replacing one now-superseded test that had asserted no restore endpoint would ever exist) plus 5 new frontend tests (acknowledgement-gating, successful restore reporting, invalid-archive error-and-retry, the resolved-path display, `fetchDataDir` mocked into the shared test fixture) — full backend suite (946/946) and full frontend suite (433/433, plus a clean production build) both re-run once after these implementation changes, per CLAUDE.md's "run the full suites only if implementation code changes" rule.

**Explicitly not built**: no cloud sync, no multi-device conflict resolution, no automatic/background migration — this is deliberately a manual, explicit, one-directional snapshot restore between two specific points in time, exactly as scoped.

**Boundary respected**: no real credential, OAuth token, or Keychain entry was ever read, displayed, or altered by any step in this entry; Bernardo's real `~/JarvisData` was never the restore *target* of any test in this session — every restore against real data in this drill used the production `/api/export` *read* path only, and every actual database replacement happened either inside an isolated `/tmp` directory or inside the backend test suite's own isolated fixture; no OAuth flow was initiated; no Hermes configuration was changed; no Git operation.

## D106 — Post-Stage-1 real-use stabilization: seven defects found by Bernardo actually using the installed app

**Context**: direct continuation after D104/D105 shipped Jarvis.app. Rather than a planned acceptance drill, Bernardo simply started using the real installed app day-to-day and reported what broke, in the order encountered: new icons wanted, an invisible tray icon, repeated Keychain prompts, a silently-broken "Connect" button, a cosmetic request for the OAuth callback page's diagnostic ring, a "VOICE ERROR" screen with no way to leave short of quitting the app, microphone access silently and permanently denied, the same voice-error screen still not reliably dismissible plus a stale-error-reappears bug, and finally Space held too long scrolling the whole app. Every defect below was root-caused with real evidence (Rust-side logs, `codesign -d --entitlements`, `ps`/`lsof`, the actual `DOMException` once it was no longer being swallowed) before being fixed — never guessed at. Full detail, file-by-file, is in `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §24; this entry is the narrative record of what happened and why each fix is correct.

**Icons**: dock/app icon regenerated from Bernardo-supplied artwork after a "double border" artifact was root-caused to ~54px of transparent padding around already-rounded source art (macOS 11+ backs non-full-bleed icons with its own plate) — fixed by cropping to content bounds and rescaling to a full-bleed 1024×1024 canvas. The tray icon was completely invisible with Tauri's `icon_as_template(true)` set, empirically confirmed broken across several rebuild cycles on this icon/OS combination; fixed by dropping that flag and using the sharper @2× source. Root cause of the template-flag failure itself was not pursued further, given time cost against a working alternative.

**OAuth "Connect" opened nothing, with no error at all** — the most involved fix in this pass. Multiple approaches were tried and ruled out with real evidence before the actual cause was found: `tauri-plugin-shell`'s `open` command (capability scope issues, still didn't fire); a custom `open_external_url` Tauri command, fully wired end-to-end (ACL, `build.rs` registration, verified against the generated `gen/schemas/*.json` files) and confirmed via added `log::info!` calls to receive **zero invocations ever**, despite the backend's own `/connect` endpoint succeeding repeatedly (up to 7 calls logged in one burst). The real cause: `window.__TAURI__` — the JS/IPC bridge — is only injected into content loaded from Tauri's own trusted origin (`offline.html`); the app's real content, reached via `window.navigate()` to `http://127.0.0.1:8000`, is treated as an external origin by Tauri regardless of being localhost, so the bridge was simply never present to call. **Fixed by moving browser-opening entirely server-side**: `backend/app/routers/integrations.py`'s new `_open_in_system_browser()` calls `subprocess.Popen(["open", url])` directly from `start_oauth`, before the endpoint returns. This works identically for the native app and `jarvisctl.sh`'s dev-mode browser workflow, and needed zero Tauri IPC — the simpler, more robust design in hindsight. All now-dead frontend/Rust code for the abandoned approach (`open_external_url`, `nativeShell.ts`'s `openExternalUrl`) was removed rather than left unreachable. A test-fixture bug was introduced and self-caught during this fix: the first version of the new `_no_real_browser_open` autouse fixture patched `app.routers.integrations.subprocess.Popen` directly — since `subprocess` is a shared module object, this broke 7 unrelated tests elsewhere that legitimately use `subprocess.Popen` (`test_hermes_profile_export.py`, both scale-benchmark tests), caught by running the full 950-test suite after the change, not reported by Bernardo. Fixed by patching the narrowly-named `_open_in_system_browser` function instead of the shared module.

**Repeated Keychain re-prompting on every integration sync**: diagnosed as two distinct, layered causes via a careful, safety-gated process (Bernardo's own detailed diagnostic instructions explicitly forbade calling `SecItemUpdate` against real tokens without disposable-item proof first, and explicitly forbade this agent from ever touching Keychain ACLs directly). (1) A real Keychain ACL configuration gap — resolved by Bernardo himself, directly, in Keychain Access.app: a temporary, explicitly-labeled-as-diagnostic-only "Allow all applications" test on both items confirmed the mechanism, then he added `/Applications/Jarvis.app/Contents/MacOS/jarvis-backend` to "Always allow" on each item and reverted the temporary setting — a user-performed real Keychain change, not a code fix. (2) A structural cause that remains, by Bernardo's own explicit choice: `keyring.set_password()` deletes and recreates the Keychain item on every token write instead of updating in place, resetting the item's ACL every time an OAuth token refreshes — meaning a re-prompt can still recur after any token refresh even with the Keychain configured correctly. This agent attempted a `SecItemUpdate`-based in-place rewrite, tested it only against a disposable, uniquely-named test item, found it failed with `errSecParam` (-50) across several isolation attempts, and fully reverted it without ever touching real tokens — judged too risky to deploy unproven against Bernardo's real credentials within this session's time. Presented with the choice, Bernardo explicitly chose to accept the occasional re-prompt over pursuing the fix further right now.

**Diagnostic ring animation**: Bernardo asked for the OAuth callback page's ring to behave "like a gear" — continuously spinning on success, and on failure turning partway and stopping, repeatedly, "like a slow gear." First implementation only played the failure animation once; Bernardo corrected this ("does that continuously as well") and it was fixed to loop indefinitely with a smooth turn-hold-return cycle, both variants respecting `prefers-reduced-motion`.

**A "VOICE ERROR" screen with no way to leave except quitting the entire app** — a two-layered bug, both layers necessary to actually fix it. Layer 1: all three Escape-key handlers (`App.tsx`, `DomainView.tsx`, `GeneralConversation.tsx`) only treated `voiceState === "listening"` as something Escape could cancel, never `"error"`. Fixing only this was insufficient. Layer 2, the real root cause: `useVoiceCapture.ts`'s own `cancel()` function had an independent hard gate, `if (voiceState !== "listening") return;`, so calling it from an `"error"` state was silently a no-op regardless of what called it — Escape, a click, anything. Both layers fixed together; covered by a genuine, unmocked regression test exploiting jsdom's natural lack of `getUserMedia` (`frontend/src/useVoiceCapture.test.ts`) rather than a mocked assertion.

**Microphone access silently and permanently denied, with Jarvis never even appearing in System Settings → Privacy & Security → Microphone** — confirmed by a screenshot showing every other mic-using app listed except Jarvis. The generic error message itself was the first thing standing in the way of diagnosing this: `usePushToTalk.ts` had a bare `catch {}` discarding the real `DOMException` entirely. Fixed to surface `err.name`/`err.message`, rebuilt, and the real error was finally visible: `NotAllowedError: ... not allowed by the user agent or the platform in the current context`. That wording — a platform-level block, not a user denial — pointed at hardened runtime: `entitlements.plist` (D104) had disabled Library Validation but never declared `com.apple.security.device.audio-input`, which is required under hardened runtime for `getUserMedia` to ever reach macOS's TCC layer at all. Without it, WKWebView refuses the request before any permission prompt exists and before Jarvis is TCC-registered at all — exactly matching both symptoms. Fixed by adding the entitlement, verified present in the actual signed binary via `codesign -d --entitlements :-` before deploying (not assumed from source), then confirmed working end-to-end against the real Mac: a genuine permission prompt appeared, and a real transcription ("TRANSCRIBING LOCALLY") succeeded.

**The voice-error overlay still had no reliable dismissal, plus two related bugs found from evidence in Bernardo's own screenshots**: Bernardo reported the error "persists, doesn't leave the app, even if I press domains, or press anywhere else." `VoiceCaptureOverlay.tsx` was `pointer-events: none` in every state (deliberately, so a push-to-talk release always reaches the real control underneath) — meaning there had never been any way to dismiss an error by clicking at all. Fixed narrowly: only the `"error"` state now accepts pointer events, with a click anywhere on it *and* a real focusable "Dismiss" button (added as a second, guaranteed hit target after the click-anywhere approach alone wasn't confirmed reliable) both calling `cancel()`. Investigating "even if I press domains" surfaced a second, real bug: navigating away from Home only ever *masked* the ambient voice overlay's display (`ambientVoiceEligible` gating what's shown) — the underlying `useVoiceCapture` state in `App.tsx` kept whatever it was, `"error"` included, so returning to Home later could show the exact same stale error again with no new failure behind it at all. Fixed with an effect that calls `ambientVoice.cancel()` the instant Home/Centre eligibility turns false, so Home always starts clean. A third bug was spotted directly in Bernardo's own screenshot (a "PATH" domain tooltip frozen open while the error was showing): the error overlay briefly capturing pointer events blocked the hovered domain node underneath from ever receiving its own pointer-leave. Fixed by having `Home.tsx` explicitly clear its hover state via a new `voiceActive` prop the moment the overlay appears.

**Holding Space too long scrolled the whole app** — root cause: in all three push-to-talk Space-key listeners, `event.preventDefault()` was called only on the very first keydown (`!event.repeat`), never on the OS's auto-repeat keydown events that fire continuously while a key stays held — so once repeat kicked in on a long hold, the browser's native "Space scrolls the page" action activated unopposed. Fixed by calling `preventDefault()` unconditionally on every Space keydown while still gating the actual `start()`/`startPushToTalk()` call behind `!event.repeat`, so a long hold no longer restarts capture or scrolls the page.

**Also**: at Bernardo's request, the voice-capture overlay's background scrim was deepened (`rgba(5,3,8,0.55)` → `rgba(4,2,7,0.9)`) so Home's domain ring and briefing strip genuinely fade away behind the core/waveform during any active voice state, rather than staying legible underneath a faint tint.

**Process**: every fix in this pass went through the same real build/deploy/verify cycle — frontend build → PyInstaller sidecar rebuild when backend code changed → signed Tauri build → quit the running app (`osascript -e 'tell application "Jarvis" to quit'`, never a raw `pkill`) → replace `/Applications/Jarvis.app` → relaunch → reconfirm `/api/health` and `/api/integrations` (both Google connections stayed `connected` with unchanged scopes throughout the entire pass, across roughly a dozen rebuild/redeploy cycles). Two full clean rebuilds (`rm -rf dist`, PyInstaller `--distpath`/`--workpath` wiped, `cargo clean`) were performed at Bernardo's explicit request to rule out stale-artifact concerns, each reverified against the live app afterward.

**Testing**: full backend suite reconfirmed at 950/950 (23 tests in `test_integrations_api.py` covering the server-side browser-open and both diagnostic-ring animation classes); full frontend suite reconfirmed at 441/441 across this pass, including two new test files (`VoiceCaptureOverlay.test.tsx`'s click-anywhere/explicit-button/never-intercepts-while-listening cases, `useVoiceCapture.test.ts`'s stuck-error-clears-via-cancel case) — both genuinely exercising the real code paths, not asserting against mocks of the fixed behavior.

**Boundary respected**: no OAuth flow was initiated or altered by this agent at any point; every real Keychain ACL change was performed directly by Bernardo himself in Keychain Access.app, never by this agent; the one Keychain-adjacent code change attempted (`SecItemUpdate`) was tested only against a disposable, uniquely-named item structurally unable to collide with real credentials, and was fully reverted without ever reading, displaying, or altering a real token; no Hermes configuration was changed; no packaging scope was expanded beyond bug-fixing already-shipped Stage 1 behavior — this is explicitly not a Stage 2 and introduces no new product feature; no Git operation of any kind.

## D107 — Local transcription latency: diagnose-first pass; one real fix kept, one attempted fix reverted after it caused a severe real-Mac incident

**Context**: Bernardo asked whether the local transcription delay he was noticing was "just how the system works" and whether a different local model would be faster, then separately asked for a focused, diagnose-before-changing-anything investigation into `faster-whisper` latency specifically — explicitly scoped as diagnostic/optimization, not feature work, with local-only speech kept non-negotiable (no hosted STT, no always-on listening, no wake word) and no touching of OAuth/Keychain/scopes/routines/schedules/real data/Home glyphs/packaging/Git.

**Inspection findings** (`app/voice/faster_whisper_stt.py`, `app/routers/voice.py`, `app/config.py`, `app/main.py`): the model already loaded lazily and was reused across calls (`self._model` cached on the instance, never reconstructed per request) — the earlier assumption in this project's own session history that it might be "reloading per request" was wrong and is corrected here. Configuration: `model_size="base"`, `device="cpu"`, `compute_type="int8"`, default decoding (`beam_size=5`, no VAD filter). `ctranslate2` (the actual inference backend behind `faster-whisper`) has no Metal/GPU device option on Apple Silicon at all — only `cpu`/`cuda` — so "GPU acceleration" was never actually an available knob here; `cpu`+`int8` is already the correct, fastest legitimate choice for this hardware. Raw audio handling (temp file write, `finally`-block deletion) was already correct and not a meaningful latency source.

**Measurement, before changing anything**: a small benchmark script (`/tmp`-scoped, never committed) loaded the real model and ran it against three short fictional sentences synthesized locally via the already-installed `edge-tts` (no real microphone audio recorded or preserved, per Bernardo's explicit instruction) — representative ~2s/~5s/~10s spoken lengths, run on the real M2 Pro:

| Stage | Time |
|---|---|
| `import faster_whisper` | 0.306s |
| `WhisperModel("base", cpu, int8)` construction | 0.929s |
| Cold first `transcribe()` (10s clip, right after construction) | 1.068s |
| Warm `transcribe()`, beam_size=5 (default), 2s/5s/10s clips | 0.664s / 0.825s / 0.982-1.012s |
| Warm `transcribe()`, beam_size=1 | 0.608s / 0.786s / 0.760s |
| Warm `transcribe()`, `vad_filter=True` | 0.672s / 0.840s / 1.120s |

**Real bottleneck, established from this data**: warm per-turn transcription (0.6-1.0s) was already reasonable and is not the dominant avoidable cost. Two structural inefficiencies were identified instead: (1) the ~0.9s model-construction cost landing on whichever push-to-talk turn happened to be first after each app launch, and (2) `POST /api/voice/transcribe` running that same blocking, CPU-bound `transcribe()` call directly inside an `async def` FastAPI handler, starving the event loop for the full duration of every transcription. `beam_size=1` and `vad_filter=True` were measured as available further levers but not adopted — the beam-size win (~150-250ms) carries a real decoding-robustness trade-off, and `vad_filter=True` measured *slower* on these short clips.

**Fix 1 (kept): non-blocking transcription.** `app/routers/voice.py`'s `transcribe` endpoint now calls `await asyncio.to_thread(stt.transcribe, path)` instead of calling it directly — the blocking inference no longer stalls the event loop. Zero change to wall-clock transcription time or to `model_size`/`device`/`compute_type`/decoding settings; only removes an avoidable side effect. Verified safe against the real packaged app (see incident below — this fix was never implicated in it).

**Fix 2 (attempted, then fully reverted): eager model preload at startup.** `FasterWhisperSTT.preload()` was added and wired into `app/main.py`'s lifespan (via `asyncio.to_thread`, gated on a new `whisper_preload_at_startup` setting) to move the ~0.9s construction cost from a user-visible first turn into the app's own startup window. This was deployed to `/Applications/Jarvis.app` and immediately caused a real, severe incident on Bernardo's Mac: an "infinite" macOS Keychain permission-prompt loop for Google Calendar access that would not stop even when he repeatedly pressed Deny.

**Incident response**: the running app was quit and its entire process tree force-killed (`osascript -e 'tell application "Jarvis" to quit'` first, then a verified-PID force-kill once that proved insufficient) to stop the active disruption immediately. Root cause was then established through controlled, isolated reproduction — a disposable `JARVIS_DATA_DIR`, the real installed sidecar binary run directly outside Tauri, no real credentials ever involved in any repro step:

* With eager preload enabled, the app entered a self-sustaining loop: a new full process re-executing the entire frozen binary roughly once per second, indefinitely — each cycle running the complete FastAPI lifespan (migrations, the Phase 10 scheduler's startup catch-up, and — in the real non-isolated case — a fresh Google Calendar Keychain access attempt) before failing to bind port 8000 (already held by the first successful instance) and exiting. 39+ such cycles were observed in under a minute with nothing external driving them (Tauri's own process was confirmed not running during these isolated repros; no launchd job or LaunchAgent was involved either).
* With eager preload disabled, the identical binary ran stably for 45+ seconds with zero respawns.
* A single ordinary **lazy** transcription (triggered via a real HTTP request, synthetic non-mic audio) caused exactly one bounded, harmless extra process/bind-failure — not a loop — confirming the defect is specifically tied to constructing `WhisperModel` *during the app's own startup lifespan*, before the server begins listening, not to model construction in general.

**Given this, the eager-preload optimization was fully reverted**, not merely disabled: `FasterWhisperSTT.preload()`, the `whisper_preload_at_startup` setting, and its lifespan wiring were all removed outright (a landmine flag left in the codebase for someone to re-enable later without this context would be worse than removing it). `tests/conftest.py`'s corresponding autouse fixture and `tests/test_faster_whisper_stt.py`'s preload-specific tests were removed along with it; the lazy-construction/reuse lifecycle tests were kept.

**A real, pre-existing packaging-level defect was found and is disclosed, not fixed, here**: the evidence strongly points to a classic PyInstaller-onefile-plus-Python-`multiprocessing` interaction (most likely triggered by `ctranslate2`'s worker-process handling on `spawn`-default macOS) — when something inside the frozen app attempts to spawn a subprocess in a way that isn't guarded for a frozen entrypoint, the "child" can end up re-executing the entire bundled program instead of the intended worker code. This risk is not fully eliminated by this revert — it is *dormant* under ordinary lazy use (confirmed bounded/harmless in the one real lazy-transcription test above) but its exact trigger conditions for turning into a sustained loop are not fully understood. Properly fixing it would mean touching the PyInstaller/Tauri packaging architecture, which Bernardo explicitly put out of scope for this task. **This is recorded here as a known open risk requiring its own dedicated investigation**, not as a solved problem.

**Testing**: 3 tests in `tests/test_faster_whisper_stt.py` (lazy construction on first use, repeated calls reuse the same instance, segment-text joining) — the preload-specific tests were removed along with the reverted feature. Full backend suite reconfirmed passing (953/953) both before and after the revert. Ruff clean on every touched file.

**Real-Mac deployment and verification**: rebuilt through the full pipeline after the revert (PyInstaller sidecar → signed Tauri build → full process-tree sweep confirming nothing was left running from any prior test or deploy cycle → replace `/Applications/Jarvis.app` → relaunch). Startup this time legitimately paused on one real, already-known (D106) Keychain re-prompt — a single one-time prompt from the scheduler's normal startup sync catch-up, not the runaway loop — which Bernardo approved directly; the app then reached healthy, both Google integrations remained `connected` with unchanged scopes, and the process tree stayed at exactly the expected count over a further 20+ second observation window. The cold-turn-then-two-warm-turns push-to-talk timing verification itself (comparing felt latency, not process stability) remains outstanding — that step requires a real microphone, which no agent session can operate, and is Bernardo's own direct action to perform and report back whenever convenient.

**Boundary respected**: faster-whisper remains fully local — no hosted STT, no new provider, no always-on listening, no wake word; cancellation, temporary-audio deletion, and every existing voice-state transition are unchanged. No OAuth flow was initiated by this agent; the one real Keychain prompt encountered during real-Mac deployment was approved by Bernardo himself, not by this agent, consistent with this project's standing rule that only Bernardo interacts with macOS security dialogs. No integration scope, routine, schedule, or real stored data was touched. No Home glyph was touched. Packaging architecture was investigated (to find the root cause) but not modified — the disclosed defect above is deliberately left as a known risk for a future, explicitly-scoped pass rather than patched under this task's boundary. No Git operation of any kind.

## D108 — The real fix for recurring Keychain prompts: in-place `SecItemUpdate` instead of `keyring`'s delete-then-recreate

**Context**: immediately after the D107 incident, Bernardo asked again how to deal with the recurring Keychain password prompt on every integration sync — the same issue D106 had diagnosed and, at the time, left as an accepted trade-off after an earlier `SecItemUpdate` attempt failed with `errSecParam` (-50) against a disposable test item and was reverted. This entry replaces that trade-off with a real, working fix, found by inspecting the actual installed `keyring` library's source rather than guessing further.

**Root cause, confirmed by reading `keyring`'s own macOS backend source** (`keyring/backends/macOS/api.py`): `set_generic_password()` unconditionally calls `delete_generic_password()` then `SecItemAdd()` — a genuine delete-and-recreate on every single write, not an update. Deleting and recreating a Keychain item resets its Access Control list, wiping any "Always Allow" grant, which is why a prompt could recur after every OAuth token refresh even with the Keychain configured correctly.

**Why the earlier `SecItemUpdate` attempt failed, and what actually fixed it**: a fresh, careful reproduction against a disposable, uniquely-named test item (never the real `jarvis.google_calendar`/`jarvis.google_health` items) reproduced the identical `errSecParam` failure at first, using the same query-dictionary shape `keyring`'s own code uses. The actual cause: `SecItemUpdate`'s `attributesToUpdate` dictionary requires a genuine `CFDataRef` for `kSecValueData` — `keyring.backends.macOS.api.create_query`'s generic value-coercion helper produces a `CFStringRef` instead, which `SecItemAdd` silently tolerates but `SecItemUpdate` rejects outright. Building the value as a real `CFDataRef` (via `CFDataCreate`) instead of routing it through that shared string-coercion path made the identical `SecItemUpdate` call succeed (status 0), with the value genuinely updated in place — confirmed by reading it back, and independently reconfirmed absent after cleanup via the `security` CLI (bypassing `keyring` and this project's own code entirely).

**The fix**: new module `app/keychain_update.py` (`update_generic_password_in_place`) — the same ctypes/Security-framework bindings `keyring`'s own macOS backend uses, plus the one binding it doesn't need (`SecItemUpdate`), built with a correct `CFDataRef` value. Returns `True` if an existing item was updated in place, `False` if no such item exists yet (nothing to update), and raises `KeychainUpdateNotSupported` on a non-macOS platform or if `keyring`'s macOS bindings are unavailable. `app/credential_store.py`'s `KeychainCredentialStore.set()` now tries this first; only falls back to `keyring.set_password()` (the original delete-recreate path) when there's no existing item to update (a genuinely new credential has no ACL to preserve yet) or the platform doesn't support it — a real Keychain error from the update attempt itself is never silently swallowed into a fallback.

**Verified against a real, disposable, uniquely-named Keychain item before touching anything real** (per Bernardo's own standing instruction, and only after his explicit approval to proceed with this specific fix): created via the real `KeychainCredentialStore.set()` fallback path, updated twice more via the same `set()` call (now taking the in-place path), each write's value confirmed correct via `KeychainCredentialStore.get()`; a direct call to `update_generic_password_in_place` also confirmed it returns `True` for an existing item; cleanup confirmed via both `KeychainCredentialStore.get()` and an independent `security find-generic-password` CLI check (exit code 44, genuinely absent). **Zero real credentials were read, displayed, enumerated, overwritten, or deleted at any point** — the test service name is timestamped and randomly suffixed, structurally unable to collide with `jarvis.google_calendar`/`jarvis.google_health`.

**Testing**: `FakeCredentialStore` (used by every other automated test in this codebase) is completely untouched — the project's standing rule that automated tests never touch the real Keychain still holds exactly as before. New tests instead mock the `app.keychain_update` seam: `KeychainCredentialStore.set()` uses the in-place path when it reports success, falls back to `keyring.set_password()` only when no item exists yet or the platform is unsupported, and never falls back on a genuine error (4 tests in `tests/test_credential_store.py`). `tests/test_keychain_update.py` covers the non-macOS platform guard. The real `SecItemUpdate` mechanism itself was proven correct via the disposable-item manual verification above, not via an automated test — consistent with the codebase's existing rule against real-Keychain automated tests. Full backend suite reconfirmed passing after this change.

**What this does not change**: `get()` and `delete()` are untouched — reads were never the problem, and a real delete (disconnecting an integration) is supposed to remove the item entirely.

**Real-world confirmation (same session, ~90 minutes of ordinary use after deployment)**: after this fix and D109's `multiprocessing.freeze_support()` fix both deployed, Bernardo reported a handful of further one-time prompts — later understood as the expected residual cost of pre-existing broken ACLs, not a failure of this fix. `google_calendar` has 5 separate Keychain items (`client_id`, `client_secret`, `access_token`, `refresh_token`, `access_token_expires_at` — confirmed via `security dump-keychain`'s account listing, metadata only, no secret values read), each with its own independent Access Control grant; `integration_service.ensure_fresh_access_token` reads 3 of them (`access_token`, `refresh_token`, `access_token_expires_at`) on *every* sync regardless of whether a refresh is needed, and only reads the other 2 (`client_id`, `client_secret`) on an actual token refresh — so each of the 5 items could only receive its one-time re-approval whenever it happened to next be read, not all at once. Once all 5 per provider had each been touched and approved once, prompts stopped. Confirmed via `GET /api/integrations`: both providers completed multiple further scheduled syncs with `last_sync_status: "ok"` and no further prompts. This is treated as real-world confirmation that the in-place-update fix holds — not merely inferred from absence of complaint.

**Boundary respected**: no real Keychain item was read, displayed, enumerated, overwritten, or deleted by any verification step — only a disposable, uniquely-named test item was touched, independently confirmed absent afterward. This fix was implemented only after passing a disposable-item test and only after Bernardo's own explicit approval to proceed, per his standing instruction. No OAuth flow was initiated. No integration scope, routine, schedule, or real stored data was touched. No Git operation of any kind.

## D109 — The real packaging defect from D107, fixed: `multiprocessing.freeze_support()`

**Context**: D107 disclosed, but explicitly did not fix, a packaging-level defect that caused a real incident (an "infinite" Keychain-prompt loop) — Bernardo then explicitly asked to investigate and fix it, rather than leave it dormant.

**Root cause, confirmed**: `backend/packaging/entrypoint.py`'s `if __name__ == "__main__":` block never called `multiprocessing.freeze_support()`. This is a standard, well-documented requirement for any PyInstaller-frozen executable that might use Python's `multiprocessing` module with the `'spawn'` start method — the default on macOS since Python 3.8. When `multiprocessing` spawns a worker, it re-invokes `sys.executable` (which, frozen, is this same bundled binary) with a special bootstrap marker in `argv`. `freeze_support()` must run first and intercepts that marker, running only the tiny worker-bootstrap code and exiting immediately — without it, a respawned "worker" instead falls through to the script's own normal entrypoint, which for this app means re-running the full FastAPI lifespan (migrations, `uvicorn.run`, the Phase 10 scheduler's startup catch-up) from scratch. This is exactly what D107 observed: whatever spawns a worker process during `WhisperModel` construction (most likely `ctranslate2`'s own worker-process handling) got a full second app instance instead of a worker, which itself repeated the same construction-and-spawn attempt — a self-sustaining, roughly-once-per-second loop, each cycle re-running the integration scheduler and, on the real Mac, hitting the Keychain again.

**The fix**: one line, `multiprocessing.freeze_support()`, added as the very first statement inside `entrypoint.py`'s `if __name__ == "__main__":` block, before `main()` is called.

**Verified against the exact original trigger condition, not just plausible reasoning**: a temporary, diagnostic-only hook was added to `entrypoint.py` (gated behind an env var, never shipped) that reconstructs a real `WhisperModel` eagerly during startup — reproducing precisely the timing that caused D107's incident. Rebuilt and run against a disposable `JARVIS_DATA_DIR`, isolated from Tauri entirely: **zero bind-conflict errors, a stable 3-process tree (bootloader, real interpreter, exactly one resource-tracker helper — not dozens), sustained for 45 seconds**, versus D107's 39+ respawns in under a minute with the identical trigger and no `freeze_support()`. The diagnostic hook was then fully removed, leaving only the one-line fix. A further clean-binary check (no diagnostic hook, ordinary lazy transcription via a real HTTP request with synthetic non-mic audio) also ran cleanly, with the resource-tracker helper staying singular rather than proliferating.

**Real-Mac deployment**: rebuilt through the full pipeline (PyInstaller sidecar → signed Tauri build → full process sweep confirming nothing left running from prior cycles → replace `/Applications/Jarvis.app` → relaunch). Reached healthy with exactly the expected process count, both Google integrations remained `connected` with unchanged scopes and `last_sync_status: ok`, and the process tree stayed stable over a further 25-second observation window with no re-spawn and no Keychain prompt this time.

**Scope note**: this is a one-line bug fix to an existing packaging file already covered by CLAUDE.md §4a's native-packaging rules — not a new packaging approach, not a technology change, and not the kind of "materially change scope" decision that section reserves for explicit approval. Bernardo explicitly asked for this investigation and fix in this same session.

**What remains open**: whether `ctranslate2`/`faster-whisper` is definitively the component whose multiprocessing usage triggered this was inferred from timing and behavior (worker-process spawning occurring specifically around `WhisperModel` construction), not confirmed by reading `ctranslate2`'s own C++/pybind11 source — `freeze_support()` is a correct, general-purpose fix regardless of which dependency triggers it, so this uncertainty doesn't weaken the fix itself. No other code path in this app is known to use `multiprocessing`; if one is added later, this same guard already covers it.

**Boundary respected**: no OAuth flow, Keychain ACL, integration scope, routine, schedule, or real stored data was touched; every reproduction used a disposable `JARVIS_DATA_DIR`, never Bernardo's real one, until the final real-Mac deployment verification (read-only health/integration checks only). No Home glyph was touched. No Git operation of any kind.

## D110 — A systemic timezone display bug found and fixed: timestamps shown as if local when they were actually UTC

**Context**: Bernardo noticed Jarvis's displayed times looked "one hour behind" his Mac's clock and asked what timezone Jarvis runs on — a polish-pass question, asked while otherwise satisfied with V1 and before any further new feature work.

**Root cause**: the backend correctly stores every timestamp in UTC (`datetime.now(timezone.utc)`), but SQLite/SQLAlchemy strips timezone information on retrieval, so every timestamp the API actually sends looks like `"2026-09-04T12:17:40.120323"` — no `Z`, no offset. JavaScript's `Date` parser has a specific, easy-to-miss quirk here: a date-*only* string with no time component defaults to UTC when parsed, but a date-*time* string with no explicit timezone marker is parsed as **local** time instead. So every place in the frontend that did `new Date(apiTimestamp)` was silently treating an actual UTC value as if it were already in the viewer's local timezone — off by exactly the viewer's UTC offset, which for Bernardo (UTC+1) reads as "everything is an hour behind."

**Scope of the bug, found via a full frontend sweep, not just the one spot originally reported**: 5 files already had their own local date-formatting helper, each independently reimplementing the same `new Date(iso).toLocaleString(...)` call and each carrying the identical bug (`BriefingStrip.tsx`, `MissionFocusRail.tsx`, `RecallCentre.tsx`, `ResearchCentre.tsx`, `DecisionCentre.tsx`). A further 8 locations across `IntegrationsCentre.tsx` (×4), `RoutineCentre.tsx` (×3), `ActionsCentre.tsx`, `MemoryItemCard.tsx`, `DomainView.tsx`, and `SkillsCentre.tsx` displayed the raw ISO string directly with no formatting at all — both bugs, wrong timezone and unreadable raw ISO text, fixed together in the same pass.

**The fix**: one new shared utility, `frontend/src/formatDateTime.ts`, exporting `formatDateTime(iso, fallback?)`. Internally it checks whether the incoming string already carries a timezone marker (`Z` or a `±HH:MM` offset) and appends `Z` itself when it doesn't, before constructing the `Date` — correctly anchoring the value to UTC before converting to the viewer's local time for display. All 5 duplicated local formatters now delegate to this one function instead of reimplementing it; all 8 raw-display call sites now call it directly. Two deliberately left untouched, having been checked and found not to have this bug: `api.ts`'s Mission Control elapsed-time calculation (`computeElapsedSeconds`) computes a *difference* between two timestamps, so the same misinterpretation on both sides cancels out; `Home.tsx`'s `target_at` inside the Mission-Focus "Discuss with Jarvis" payload is machine-readable content sent to the model for reasoning, not something a person reads directly, so raw ISO there is actually correct.

**Testing**: `frontend/src/formatDateTime.test.ts` (4 new tests) — a timezone-less timestamp formats identically to the same instant with an explicit `Z`, and already-timezoned strings (`Z` suffix or a numeric offset) are never double-suffixed into an `Invalid Date`. One existing test (`ActionsCentre.test.tsx`) had been asserting against placeholder timestamp strings (`"t1"`, `"t2"`) that were never valid dates to begin with; updated to use real ISO timestamps so the assertion reflects realistic data rather than accidentally depending on the previous no-op raw-string behavior. Full frontend suite reconfirmed at 445/445, typecheck clean, production build clean.

**Boundary respected**: backend untouched entirely — this is a pure frontend display fix; the backend's own choice to store everything in UTC is correct and unchanged. No OAuth flow, Keychain, integration scope, routine, schedule, or real stored data was touched. No Home glyph or packaging architecture was touched. No Git operation of any kind.

## D111 — Home.tsx's async-lifecycle guard: a genuine Strict Mode bug found and fixed, three other Centre components proven clean

**Context**: a targeted follow-up to close two items the earlier audit pass (which produced the D110-adjacent `mountedRef` fix) left unverified: whether that fix was actually safe under React Strict Mode, and whether the three Centre components flagged as the same code-smell pattern (`IntegrationsCentre.tsx`, `RoutineCentre.tsx`, `SkillsCentre.tsx`) had a real, reproducible problem or only a superficial resemblance.

**A genuine defect found in the fix itself**: `mountedRef` was only ever set to `false` (on cleanup), never explicitly reset to `true` on effect setup. React 18 Strict Mode's dev-only mount→cleanup→remount cycle runs that cleanup once before a component's real lifetime begins — without the explicit reset, `mountedRef.current` stayed permanently `false` for the rest of Home's real mounted lifetime, silently dropping every legitimate `loadCurrentMission`/`loadMissionCandidates`/`loadBriefing` update. Proven with a real red/green test: a deterministic test using controllable (deferred) promises failed reliably with the bug present (a briefing update never reached the screen) and passed after adding `mountedRef.current = true` at the top of the effect.

**What the original crash actually required, established by direct experiment, not assumption**: the specific incident this whole guard exists for (`ReferenceError: window is not defined`, intermittent across full-suite runs) requires vitest's jsdom environment for an entire test *file* to be torn down while a promise from an earlier file is still pending — not merely component unmount. With every `mountedRef` guard temporarily stripped back out, the new deterministic unmount test still passed cleanly, proving that specific crash can't be reproduced by unmounting alone in one still-alive test environment; a real browser tab's `window` never disappears while the page is open, so it is a test-infrastructure artifact rather than a producible bug. The `mountedRef` fix remains correct and worth keeping regardless — it prevents real wasted/incorrect state updates on an unmounted component — but this is recorded so the new regression test's actual guarantees (interval cleanup, no synchronous throw, no console error) aren't overstated.

**The three other Centre components**: each given the identical deterministic treatment (a deferred promise for their primary mount-effect fetch, mount → unmount → resolve after) rather than being fixed speculatively. All three passed cleanly with no guard at all. Left unchanged, per the standing rule that a suspicious pattern without a reproduced consequence is an observation, not a defect.

**Testing**: `frontend/src/Home.lifecycle.test.tsx` (2 new tests — the Strict Mode regression above, and a companion proving the guard does not suppress a legitimate update while genuinely mounted) and `frontend/src/CentreLifecycle.test.tsx` (3 new tests, one per Centre component). Full frontend suite reconfirmed at 450/450, `tsc -b` clean, oxlint baseline unchanged (same 17 pre-existing warnings, none new), production build clean.

**Boundary respected**: no real data, credentials, integrations, routines, or the installed `/Applications/Jarvis.app` were touched — this was a source-only frontend fix and test addition. No Git operation of any kind.
